\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}logg=-g_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}g^{ji}.

Hence the result.

Solution 2.27: As
g_{ij}
is a symmetric tensor of rank 2 so its transformation law gives

{g^{\prime}}_{ij}=\frac{\partial x^{p}}{\partial x^{{}^{\prime}i}}\frac{\partial x^{q}}{\partial x^{{}^{\prime}j}}g_{pq}~.

Taking determinant of both sides we have

\left|g^{\prime}_{ij}\right|=\left|g_{\alpha\beta}\right|\left|\frac{\partial x^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{{}^{\prime}i}}\right|\left|\frac{\partial x^{\beta}}{\partial x^{{}^{\prime}j}}\right|

i.e,~~g^{\prime}=gJ^{2}~~,~~J=\left|\frac{\partial x^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{{}^{\prime}i}}\right|~~\mbox{is the Jacobian of transformation.}

\displaystyle\mbox{But}~~~~dx^{1}dx^{2}\ldots\ldots dx^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|J\right|dx^{\prime 1}dx^{\prime 2}\ldots\ldots dx^{\prime n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}}{g}}dx^{\prime 1}dx^{\prime 2}\ldots\ldots dx^{\prime n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\sqrt{g}dx^{1}dx^{2}\ldots\ldots dx^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{g^{\prime}}dx^{\prime 1}dx^{\prime 2}\ldots\ldots dx^{\prime n}~.

Hence
dV=\sqrt{g}dx^{1}dx^{2}\ldots\ldots dx^{n}
is an invariant volume element.

Proof 2.28: Let
A
be any square matrix having determinant
\left|\det A\right|
. We consider a variation of the elements of the matrix.Then

\displaystyle\delta\ln\left|\det A\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left|\det(A+\delta A)\right|-\ln\left|\det A\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left|\frac{\det(A+\delta A)}{\det A}\right|=\ln\left|\det A^{-1}(A+\delta A)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln\left|\det(I+A^{-1}\delta A)\right|.

Now,
\det(I+\delta)=1+\mbox{Tr}(\delta)+O(\delta^{2})
,
\delta
is a small square metric of same order as
A
.

Thus

\displaystyle\delta\ln\left|\det A\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln(1+\mbox{Tr}(A^{-1})\delta A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Tr}(A^{-1})\delta A

\displaystyle\mbox{so,}~~\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{K}}\ln\left|\det A\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Tr(A^{-1})\frac{\partial A}{\partial x^{K}}~.

In particular, if we choose
A=g_{ij}
, the metric tensor, then

\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{K}}\ln g=\frac{G^{ij}}{g}\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{K}}~,~~G^{ij}=~\mbox{Cofactor~of}~g_{ij}~\mbox{in}~g=g\cdot g^{ji}

\frac{1}{g}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{K}}=\frac{g.g^{ji}}{g}\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{K}}=g^{ji}\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{K}}~.

Solution 2.29: Let us consider a co-ordinate transformations:

x^{i}\rightarrow\overline{x}^{i}\rightarrow\overline{\overline{x}}^{i}

i.e.,~~~~\overline{x}^{i}=\overline{x}^{i}(x^{k})~\mbox{and}~\overline{\overline{x}}^{i}=\overline{\overline{x}}^{i}(\overline{x}^{k})

Let the Christoffel symbols in these co-ordinate systems be

\Gamma^{i}_{jk}~,~\overline{\Gamma}^{i}_{jk}~\mbox{and}~\overline{\overline{\Gamma}}^{i}_{jk}
respectively.

For the first set of co-ordinate transformation
(i.e.,~~x^{i}\longrightarrow\overline{x}^{i})
the transformation of the Christoffel symbols are given by equation (
2.16
) as

\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{ij}=\Gamma^{c}_{ab}\frac{\partial x^{a}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}\frac{\partial x^{b}}{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial x^{c}}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial x^{c}}~.

(2.76)

Similarly, corresponding to the second set of co-ordinate transformation

\overline{\overline{\Gamma}}^{r}_{pq}=\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{ij}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}.\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}~.

(2.77)

Now combining equation (
2.76
) and (
2.77
) we have

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\Gamma}}^{r}_{pq}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{c}_{ab}\frac{\partial x^{a}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}\frac{\partial x^{b}}{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial x^{c}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial x^{c}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}

(2.78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{c}_{ab}\frac{\partial x^{a}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial x^{b}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{c}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}~.

From the chain rule of differentiation:

\frac{\partial x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}\cdot\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}=\frac{\partial x^{c}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}~,

differentiating both sides w.r.t.
\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}
, we get

\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}+\frac{\partial x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{c}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}

Now multiplying both sides by
\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}
we get

\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{c}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}=\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}

\mbox{or,}~~~~\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{x}^{i}\partial\overline{x}^{j}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{i}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial\overline{x}^{j}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\overline{x}^{k}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial\overline{x}^{k}}=\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}~.

Using this relation in equation (
2.78
) we have

\overline{\overline{\Gamma}}^{r}_{pq}=\Gamma^{c}_{ab}\frac{\partial x^{a}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}}\frac{\partial x^{b}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{c}}{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{p}\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{q}}\frac{\partial\overline{\overline{x}}^{r}}{\partial x^{c}}

Hence the transformation law for Christoffel symbols possesses transitive property.

Solution 2.30:

\overline{\nabla}_{m}\phi=\overline{\partial}_{m}\phi=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\overline{x}_{m}}=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x^{k}}\cdot\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\overline{x}_{m}}=\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\overline{x}^{m}}(\partial_{k}\phi)

~~~~~~~~~~=\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\overline{x}^{m}}(\nabla_{k}\phi)~.

This shows that
\nabla_{k}\phi
is a (0,1) tensor.

Solution 2.31:

\overline{A}_{p}=\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}A_{i}~.

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\overline{A}_{p}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\left(\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}A_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}\partial\overline{x}^{p}}A_{i}+\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}

=\left(\overline{\Gamma}^{s}_{qp}\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{s}}-\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\right)A_{i}+\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}~~~~\mbox{(using~equation~(\ref{2.55}))}

\displaystyle=\overline{\Gamma}^{s}_{qp}\overline{A}_{s}-\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\Gamma^{s}_{ji}A_{s}+\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}

\mbox{or,}~~~~\frac{\partial\overline{A}_{p}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}-\overline{\Gamma}^{s}_{qp}\overline{A}_{s}=\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\left(\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}-\Gamma^{s}_{ji}A_{s}\right)

\mbox{or,}~~~~\overline{\nabla}_{q}\overline{A}_{p}=\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\overline{x}^{p}}\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial\overline{x}^{q}}\nabla_{j}A_{i}~.

This shows that
\nabla_{j}A_{i}
is a (0, 2) tensor.

In a similar way it can be shown that
\nabla_{k}A^{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{s}}
is a
(r,~s+1)
tensor.

Note:
Covariant differentiation, increases the covariant order of a tensor by one.

Solution 2.32: By the definition of Christoffel symbol

\Gamma_{mij}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{m}}+\frac{\partial g_{mj}}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial g_{mi}}{\partial x^{j}}\right)

and,

\Gamma_{mij}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{m}}+\frac{\partial g_{im}}{\partial x^{j}}-\frac{\partial g_{mj}}{\partial x^{i}}\right).

Adding and noting the symmetry of
g_{ij}
we get

\partial_{m}g_{ij}=\Gamma_{mij}+\Gamma_{mji}~.

Solution 2.33: The angle between any two hypersurfaces is equal to the angle between their normals. Now
\underline{e}^{i}
and
\underline{e}^{j}
are normals
i
-th and
j
-th co-ordinate hypersurfaces respectively, where
\{\underline{e}^{1},\underline{e}^{2},\ldots\ldots,\underline{e}^{n}\}
is the reciprocal natural basis of the given co-ordinate system.

\cos\theta_{ij}=\frac{(\underline{e}^{i}\cdot\underline{e}^{j})}{\sqrt{\underline{e}^{i}\cdot\underline{e}^{i}}\sqrt{\underline{e}^{j}\cdot\underline{e}^{j}}}~.

But we know that
\underline{e}^{i}\cdot\underline{e}^{j}=g^{ij}
, hence

\cos\theta_{ij}=\frac{g^{ij}}{\sqrt{g^{ii}}\sqrt{g^{jj}}}.

Note:
The co-ordinate hypersurfaces are orthogonal to each other iff
g^{ij}=0
whenever
i\neq j
.

Solution 2.34:

g=\det g_{ij}~.

Let
G^{ij}=
cofactor of
g_{ji}
in
g
, then
g^{ij}=\dfrac{G^{ij}}{g}
.

We know that

\displaystyle\partial_{m}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G^{ij}\partial_{m}g_{ji}=gg^{ij}\partial_{m}g_{ji}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle gg^{ij}\left(\Gamma_{mji}+\Gamma_{mij}\right)=g\left(\Gamma^{i}_{im}+\Gamma^{i}_{mi}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g\Gamma^{s}_{ms}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s}_{ms}=\frac{1}{2g}\partial_{m}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{m}\log g=\partial_{m}\log{\sqrt{g}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\partial_{m}(\sqrt{g})~.

Solution 2.35:

\displaystyle\nabla_{k}\delta^{i}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\delta^{i}_{j}+\Gamma^{i}_{ks}\delta^{s}_{j}-\Gamma^{u}_{kj}\delta^{i}_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0+\Gamma^{i}_{kj}-\Gamma^{i}_{kj}=0

\displaystyle\nabla_{k}g_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{k}g_{ij}-\Gamma^{s}_{ki}g_{sj}-\Gamma^{u}_{kj}g_{iu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{k}g_{ij}-\left(\Gamma_{kij}+\Gamma_{kji}\right)=\partial_{k}g_{ij}-\partial_{k}g_{ij}=0.

Also as
g^{ij}g_{jp}=\delta^{i}_{p}
, so
\nabla_{k}\left(g^{ij}g_{jp}\right)=0

\Rightarrow(\nabla_{k}g^{ij})g_{jp}+g^{ij}(\nabla_{k}g_{jp})=0~~~~\Rightarrow\nabla_{k}g^{ij}=0.

Solution 2.36: We know that

\nabla_{j}A^{p}_{q}=\partial_{j}A^{p}_{q}+\Gamma^{p}_{sj}A^{s}_{q}-\Gamma^{s}_{qj}A^{p}_{s}

\displaystyle\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}A^{p}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\left(\nabla_{j}A^{p}_{q}\right)-\Gamma^{\sigma}_{jk}\nabla_{\sigma}A^{p}_{q}+\Gamma^{p}_{\sigma k}\nabla_{j}A^{\sigma}_{q}-\Gamma^{\sigma}_{qk}\nabla_{j}A^{p}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\partial_{j}A^{P}_{q}+\left(\partial_{k}\Gamma^{p}_{sj}\right)A^{s}_{q}+\Gamma^{p}_{sj}\left(\partial_{k}A^{s}_{q}\right)-\left(\partial_{k}\Gamma^{s}_{qj}\right)A^{p}_{s}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Gamma^{s}_{qj}\partial_{k}A^{p}_{s}-\Gamma^{\sigma}_{jk}\left(\partial_{\sigma}A^{p}_{q}+\Gamma^{p}_{s\sigma}A^{s}_{q}-\Gamma^{s}_{q\sigma}A^{p}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Gamma^{p}_{\sigma k}\left(\partial_{j}A^{\sigma}_{q}+\Gamma^{\sigma}_{sj}A^{s}_{q}-\Gamma^{s}_{qj}A^{\sigma}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\sigma}_{qk}\left(\partial_{j}A^{p}_{\sigma}+\Gamma^{p}_{sj}A^{s}_{\sigma}-\Gamma^{s}_{\sigma j}A^{p}_{s}\right).

Commuting on
k
and
j
we get

\displaystyle(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}-\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k})A^{p}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{k}\Gamma^{p}_{js}-\partial_{j}\Gamma^{p}_{ks}+\Gamma^{p}_{\sigma k}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{js}-\Gamma^{p}_{\sigma j}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{ks}\right)A^{s}_{q}

(2.79)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{k}\Gamma^{s}_{jq}-\partial_{j}\Gamma^{s}_{kq}+\Gamma^{s}_{\sigma k}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{jq}-\Gamma^{s}_{\sigma j}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{kq}\right)A^{p}_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-R^{p}_{sjk}A^{s}_{q}+R^{s}_{qjk}A^{p}_{s}.

In a similar way

[\nabla_{k},\nabla_{j}]A_{pq}=A_{sq}R^{s}_{pjk}+A_{ps}R^{s}_{qjk}

(2.80)

and,

[\nabla_{k},\nabla_{j}]A^{pq}=-R^{p}_{sjk}A^{sq}-R^{q}_{sjk}A^{ps}.

(2.81)

Solution 2.37: Transvecting the given relation
R_{ij}=\lambda g_{ij}
by
g^{ij}
we get

R=\lambda n,~~i.e.,~~\lambda=\frac{R}{n}

Hence,

R_{ij}=\frac{R}{n}g_{ij}

A Riemannian space of dimension ‘
n
’ in which the above equation holds is called an Einstein space of dimension ‘
n
’. In Einstein’s general relativity the space-time world is a (pseudo)Riemannian 4–space of signature
(+,+,+,-)
or
(+,-,-,-)
which is an Einstein space.

Note:
As
g^{ij}g_{ik}=\delta^{i}_{k}
, so
g^{ij}~g_{ij}=\delta^{i}_{~i}=\delta^{1}_{~1}+\delta^{2}_{~2}+\ldots\ldots+\delta^{n}_{~n}=n
.

Solution 2.38: Let
n
be the dimension of the space. Now transvecting the given relation by
g^{ij}
we get

n\,R_{kl}-\delta^{j}_{l}R_{jk}+\delta^{i}_{k}~R_{il}-R\,g_{kl}=0

\mbox{or,}~~~~n\,R_{kl}-R_{lk}+R_{kl}-R\,g_{kl}=0

\mbox{or,}~~~~R_{kl}=\frac{R}{n}g_{kl}~.

Hence the space is Einstein.

Solution 2.39: Let
V_{n}
be a space of constant curvature of dimension ‘
n
’. Then we have

R_{hijk}=k\left(g_{hj}~g_{ik}-g_{hk}~g_{ij}\right)

Now multiplying by
g^{hk}
and contracting on
h
and
k
we get

R_{ij}=k\left(\delta^{k}_{~j}g_{ik}-ng_{ij}\right)=k(1-n)g_{ij}~,

showing that the space is an Einstein space.

Note:
Here
k(1-n)=\dfrac{R}{n}~~~~~\Rightarrow R=kn(1-n)
.

Solution 2.40: We have,
R^{h}_{~j}=g^{hi}R_{ij}

\displaystyle\nabla_{h}R^{h}_{~j}=g^{hi}\nabla_{h}R_{ij}=g^{hi}\nabla_{h}R^{s}_{~ijs}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{hi}\nabla_{h}\left(g^{sp}R_{pijs}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{hi}g^{sp}\nabla_{h}R_{pijs}~.

By Bianchi’s 2nd identity we have

\nabla_{h}R_{pijs}+\nabla_{j}R_{pish}+\nabla_{s}R_{pihj}=0.

So we can write

\displaystyle\nabla_{h}R^{h}_{~j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g^{hi}g^{sp}\left[\nabla_{j}R_{pish}+\nabla_{s}R_{pihj}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\nabla_{j}\left(g^{hi}g^{sp}R_{pish}\right)-\nabla_{s}\left(g^{hi}g^{sp}R_{pihj}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\left(g^{hi}g^{sp}R_{pihs}\right)-\nabla_{s}\left(g^{sp}g^{hi}R_{pihj}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\left(g^{hi}R_{ih}\right)-\nabla_{s}\left(g^{sp}R_{pj}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}R-\nabla_{s}R^{s}_{~j}=\frac{\partial R}{\partial x^{j}}-\nabla_{h}R^{h}_{~j}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\nabla_{h}R^{h}_{~j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial R}{\partial x^{j}}~.

Solution 2.41: Einstein space of dimension ‘
n
’ is defined by the relation

R^{k}_{~j}=\frac{R}{n}\delta^{k}_{~j}

\nabla_{k}R^{k}_{~j}=\frac{1}{n}\delta^{k}_{~j}\nabla_{k}R=\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial R}{\partial x^{j}}~.

But,

\nabla_{k}R^{k}_{~j}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial R}{\partial x^{j}}

so, we have
(n-2)\dfrac{\partial R}{\partial x^{j}}=0
.

Thus if
n>2
then scalar curvature
R
is a constant.

Solution 2.42: It is given that
g_{ij}=0~,~~\forall~i,~j
such that
i\neq j
.

So
g^{ij}=0~,~~\forall~i,~j
s.t.
i\neq j
.

Also
g^{ii}=\dfrac{1}{g_{ii}}~,~~\forall~i
.

From the antisymmetric property of curvature tensor

R_{hijk}=-R_{ihjk}~~~,~~~R_{hijk}=-R_{hikj}~.

Note that
i~,~j~,~h
take values from 1 to 3 and they are all unequal. So for convenience we choose
i=1~,~j=2~,~h=3
.

\displaystyle\mbox{Now,}~~~~~~~~~~R_{hj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{pq}R_{phjq}=\sum^{3}_{p=1}g^{pp}R_{phjp}=\sum^{3}_{p=1}\frac{R_{phjp}}{g_{pp}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{R_{ihji}}{g_{ii}}+\frac{R_{jhjj}}{g_{jj}}+\frac{R_{hhjh}}{g_{hh}}=\frac{R_{ihji}}{g_{jj}}=\frac{R_{hiij}}{g_{ii}}~.

\displaystyle\mbox{Also,}~~~~~~~~~~R_{hh}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum^{3}_{p=1}\frac{R_{phhp}}{g_{pp}}=\frac{R_{ihhi}}{g_{ii}}+\frac{R_{jhhj}}{g_{jj}}+\frac{R_{hhhh}}{g_{hh}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{R_{hiih}}{g_{ii}}+\frac{R_{hjjh}}{g_{ij}}~.

Chapter 3

Curves in a Riemannian Space

3.1

Parametric Representation

Let
x^{i}
be a coordinate system defined in a coordinate neighbourhood of an
n
-dimensional Riemannian space
M
. A curve in
M
is given by

x^{i}=f^{i}(u)~,~~~i=1,2,\ldots,n,

(3.1)

where
u
is a real variable, called the parameter, defined in some interval
I
of the real line
R
. If the functions
f^{i}
(
i=1,2,\ldots,n
) are
C^{\infty}
, then the curve is also briefly called a
C^{\infty}
curve. As the functions are single valued so for a single value of
u
, there corresponds a single point of the curve. If the converse is true then the parameter is also called a coordinate for the curve.

Example:
x^{1}=a\cos u
,
x^{2}=a\sin u
,
x^{3}=0~,~~\ldots~~,x^{n}=0
.

We have
P(0)=P(2\pi)
. So if we take the interval as
0\leq u<2\pi
then the correspondence between points and parametric values is one-one and the parameter become a coordinate.

Note: If in the eq. (
3.1
) of a curve
f^{i^{\prime}}(u)
are all identically zero then
f^{i}(u)
are all constants and the locus of (
3.1
) reduces to a point and then eq. (
3.1
) is not called a curve. It may however happen that
f^{i^{\prime}}(u)=(0,0,\ldots,0)
at isolated points. The parameter will be said to be irregular at such points.

3.2

Arc Length of a Curve

If
s
be the measure of the arc length of the curve (
3.1
) then from (
2.17
),
i.e.
,

ds^{2}=g_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}~,

we get

\frac{ds}{du}=\sqrt{g_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{du}\frac{dx^{j}}{du}}~.

(3.2)

On integration,

s=\int\limits_{u_{0}}^{u}\sqrt{g_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{du}\frac{dx^{j}}{du}}\,du~.

This gives the arc-length of the curve from a fixed point
A(u_{0})
to any point
P(u)
.

As
g_{ij}
is positive definite, so it follows from (
3.2
) that
\dfrac{ds}{du}\neq 0
. Hence from

\frac{dx^{i}}{du}=\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{ds}{du}~,

it follows that
\dfrac{dx^{i}}{du}\neq 0
which implies
\dfrac{dx^{i}}{ds}\neq 0
and vice-versa. Also
\dfrac{ds}{du}\neq 0
shows that the correspondence between
u
and
s
is one-one and so if
u
is a coordinate for the curve then
s
is also so. Further, the correspondence being one-one,
u
is a function of
s
and
s
is a function of
u
,
i.e.
,
u=\phi(s)
. Then the equation of the curve can be written as

x^{i}=f^{i}(\phi(s))=g^{i}(s)~~~~\mbox{(say)}.

Therefore,
s
is also a parameter of the curve.

3.3

Tangent and Normal to the Curve

The unit tangent vector
t
to the curve in
M
at any point
P
is given by the contravariant components

t^{i}=\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}~,~~~i=1,2,\ldots,n~.

Note: For any general parameter
u
, the vector
\frac{dx^{i}}{du}
is also called a tangent vector but it is not a unit vector. As
t
is a unit vector so

g_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}=1.

Taking intrinsic differentiation along the curve, we have

g_{ij}t^{i}\frac{\delta t^{j}}{ds}=0.

The above relation shows that (assuming
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}}{ds}
is non-zero) the vector
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}}{ds}
is normal to the tangent
t
. The unit vector corresponding to this vector is called the principal normal or the first normal to the curve. The magnitude of the vector
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}}{ds}
is called the first curvature of the curve relative to
M
. It is denoted by
\kappa_{1}
,
i.e.
,
\kappa_{1}=\left|\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}}{ds}\right|
.

Note: The idea of curvature comes from the Euclidean space and we shall show it in the following corollary.

Corollary: Show that
\left|\dfrac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}\right|
, where
t
is the unit tangent vector to any curve
\gamma
in
n
-dimensional space, under rectangular Cartesian coordinates gives the curvature of the curve (
i.e.
, the arc-rate of turning of the tangent).

Proof: Let
t
be the unit tangent vector at any point
P(s)
on the curve and
\textit{{t}}+\Delta\textit{{t}}
be the tangent vector at the neighbouring point
Q(s+\Delta s)
. Suppose
\Delta\theta
be the angle between these tangents at
P
and
Q
.Now through any point
O
, we draw
\textit{{OA}}=\textit{{t}}
and
\textit{{OB}}=\textit{{t}}+\Delta\textit{{t}}
. As
OA=\left|\textit{{t}}\right|=1
and
OB=\left|\textit{{t}}+\Delta\textit{{t}}\right|=1
, so the perpendicular
ON
bisects
AB
as well as
\angle AOB
. From vector algebra,
\textit{{AB}}=\Delta\textit{{t}}
. As
\angle AOB=\Delta\theta
, so
\angle NOB=\dfrac{1}{2}\Delta\theta
and
\textit{{NB}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\Delta\textit{{t}}
.

Fig. 3.1

Now from the triangle
NBO
, we have

\displaystyle\sin\frac{1}{2}\Delta\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{NB}{OB}=\frac{\left|\frac{1}{2}\Delta\textit{{t}}\right|}{1}

\displaystyle i.e.~~~~\frac{\sin\frac{1}{2}\Delta\theta}{\frac{1}{2}\Delta\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\Delta\textit{{t}}\right|}{\left|\Delta\theta\right|}

Now proceeding to the limit as
\Delta s\longrightarrow 0
(
i.e.
,
Q\longrightarrow P
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{d\theta}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\mbox{Thus,}~~\left|\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{d\theta}\right|\left|\frac{d\theta}{ds}\right|=\kappa

Note: The result
\left|\dfrac{d\textit{{t}}}{d\theta}\right|=1
implies that if we consider any vector field of unit magnitude then the intrinsic derivative of that vector field will also be a unit vector if the intrinsic derivative is taken with respect to the angle
\theta
in polar coordinates.

3.4

Serret
-
Frenet formulae for a curve in a Riemannian space

Let
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
be the unit tangent vector to a curve
\gamma
in
M
at
P
and ‘
s
’ is the arc length along the curve
\gamma
from a fixed point to
P
. The derived vector
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}
is identically zero throughout the curve if the curve is a geodesic. So we assume
\gamma
to be a non-geodesic curve and hence
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}\neq 0
in general.

As
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
is of constant magnitude (unit length) so
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}
is normal to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
(
i.e.
, normal to the curve
\gamma
). Let us write

\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}=\kappa_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}

(3.3)

where
\kappa_{1}
is the magnitude of
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}
, called the first curvature and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
is the corresponding unit vector. We call
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}
the first curvature vector and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
the first normal or principal normal or the second orthonormalized osculating vector.

Next we consider the derived vector
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}
of
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
at
P
. Suppose
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}\neq 0
and resolve it into two components, one in the plane of
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
and the other normal to this plane. The latter is denoted by
\kappa_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
, where
\kappa_{2}
is its magnitude and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
is the corresponding unit vector. Since
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
is of constant magnitude, so
\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}
is perpendicular to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
. Hence we write

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}=\sigma_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}+\kappa_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}~,

where the scalar
\sigma_{1}
is given by
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
.

As
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}=0
, so taking intrinsic derivative with respect to the arc length
s
, we have

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}+\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}=0.

But using (
3.3
), we have

\sigma_{1}=\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}=-\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}\cdot\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{1}~.

Thus,

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}+\kappa_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}~.

(3.4)

We call
\kappa_{2}
the second curvature scalar and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
the second normal or the third orthonormalized osculating vector.

We assume the derivative
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}
of
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
to be a non-zero vector and resolve it into two components, one in the 3-plane of
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
and the other normal to this plane which we denote by
\kappa_{3}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{4}}
. As
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
is a vector of constant magnitude so
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}
will be orthogonal to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
and hence the former component will be a linear combination of
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
. So let us write

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}=\rho_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}+\rho_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}+\kappa_{3}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{4}}~,

where
\rho_{1}=\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}~~,~~\rho_{2}=\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
.

As
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}=0
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}=0
, so by intrinsic differentiation

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}+\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}=0~~~~~~~~~~,~~~~~~~~~~\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}+\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}}{ds}=0

~~~~~~~~~~~~i.e.,~~\rho_{1}+\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}\kappa\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}=0~~~~~~~~~~~~,~~~~~i.e.,~~\rho_{2}+\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}\left(-\kappa_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}+\kappa_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}\right)=0~~~~

i.e.,~~\rho_{1}=0~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~,~~~~~i.e.,~~\rho_{2}=-\kappa_{2}~.~~~~~~~~~~~~

Hence we get the relation

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}+\kappa_{3}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{4}}~.

(3.5)

We continue the process until we obtain the relation

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-2}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{n-3}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-3}}+\kappa_{n-2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}~.

(3.6)

Then we define
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
as an unit vector perpendicular to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}~,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}
and so directed as to make
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}~,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
a right-handed basis of the tangent space to
M
at
P
. So the next equation may be put in the form

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{n-2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-2}}+\kappa_{n-1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}~.

(3.7)

It should be noted that although
\kappa_{1}~,~\kappa_{2}~,\ldots,~\kappa_{n-2}
are all positive,
\kappa_{n-1}
may be of any sign. Since there cannot be any vector orthogonal to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}~,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
, so the equation for
\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}}{ds}
will be

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{n-1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}~.

(3.8)

The set of all these intrinsic derivative equations can be written in compact form as

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa_{r-1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r-1}}+\kappa_{r}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r+1}}~~,~r=2,3,\ldots,n-1

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa_{n-1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}

(3.9)

or in a more compact form

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{l}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{l-1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{l-1}}+\kappa_{l}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{l+1}}~~,~l=1,2,\ldots,n

(3.10)

with
\kappa_{0}=0=\kappa_{n}
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n+1}}=\overrightarrow{0}
.

These formulae are known as Serret-Frenet formulae or simply Frenet formulae for the curve
\gamma
in
M
.

Corollary I: For three dimensional Euclidean space
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{3}}
are respectively written as
t
,
n
,
b
and are called the tangent, the principal normal and the binormal vectors respectively. Also
\kappa_{1}
,
\kappa_{2}
are generally written as
\kappa
,
\tau
and are called the curvature and the torsion respectively. So the Frenet formulae under rectangular Cartesian coordinates take the form

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{n}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{b}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tau\textit{{n}}

(3.11)

It may be noted that
\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{n}}=\textit{{b}}
,
\textit{{n}}\times\textit{{b}}=\textit{{t}}
and
\textit{{b}}\times\textit{{t}}=\textit{{n}}
.

At each point of the curve, the planes spanned by
\{\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}}\}
,
\{\textit{{t}},\textit{{b}}\}
and
\{\textit{{n}},\textit{{n}}\}
are respectively known as osculating plane, rectifying plane and normal plane.

One can define a vector in the rectifying plane as
\textit{{d}}=\tau\textit{{t}}+\kappa\textit{{b}}
and it is termed as Darboux vector. Here the term
\kappa\textit{{b}}
represents the rate of turning about the binormal vector due to curvature while the term
\tau\textit{{t}}
stands for the rate of turning about the tangent vector due to torsion. Further, one can write the above Serret-Frenet formulae (i.e., eq. (
3.4
)) compactly as

\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}=\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{d}},~~\frac{d\textit{{n}}}{ds}=\textit{{n}}\times\textit{{d}}\mbox{~~and~~}\frac{d\textit{{b}}}{ds}=\textit{{b}}\times\textit{{d}}.

(3.12)

If
\textit{{r}}=\textit{{r}(s)}
be a curve in
E^{3}
, (assuming at least four continuous derivatives), then

\textit{{t}}=\dfrac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}

is the tangent vector of length unity.

If a particles moves along the curve such that one can identify time as the arc length then

\textit{{v}}=\textit{{t}}\mbox{~and~}|\textit{{v}}|=1

then the curve is known as unit speed curve.

Now

\frac{d\textit{{v}}}{d\tau}=\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}=\kappa\textit{{n}}

where
\tau
is identified as the time coordinate. So the acceleration of the particle is along the normal (as
|\textit{{v}}|=1
so
\dfrac{d|\textit{{v}}|}{d\tau}=0
and
\dfrac{v^{2}}{\rho}=\dfrac{1}{\rho}=\kappa
).

Thus if a particle moves along a unit speed curve then its acceleration is always along the principal normal of magnitude
\kappa
, the curvature of the curve at the point.

\bullet
We shall now determine the conditions for which the position vector (P.V.) of a particle moving in a space curve is always on the osculating plane

As the P.V. lies on the osculating plane, so we write

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(s)\textit{{t}}(s)+g(s)\textit{{n}}(s)

\displaystyle\mbox{i.e,~}\textit{{t}}=\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+g^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+a\kappa\textit{{n}}+g\left(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow(1-a^{\prime}+\kappa g)\textit{{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a\kappa+g^{\prime})\textit{{n}}+g\tau\textit{{b}}

\Rightarrow\tau=0
i.e.
\textit{{b}}=
constant vector and hence the curve is a plane curve. Also we have

\frac{1-a^{\prime}}{g}=\frac{g^{\prime}}{a},~~a\kappa+g^{\prime}=0

\Rightarrow a^{2}+g^{2}=2F(s),~a=f(s)
with
f(s)=\dfrac{dF}{ds}

\therefore|\textit{{r}}|^{2}=2F(s)
and
\kappa=\dfrac{\frac{d^{2}F}{ds^{2}}-1}{\sqrt{2F(s)-\left(\frac{dF}{ds}\right)^{2}}}

Here
F(s)
is an arbitrary differentiable (at least twice) +ve function of
s
.

\bullet
Conditions for P.V. to be on rectifying plane:

Let
~~~\textit{{r}}(s)=\lambda(s)\textit{{t}}(s)+\mu(s)\textit{{b}}(s)

So
\textit{{t}}=\dfrac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}=\lambda^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+\mu^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+\lambda\kappa\textit{{n}}-\mu\tau\textit{{n}}

\Rightarrow\lambda^{\prime}(s)=1,~\lambda\kappa=\mu\tau,~\mu^{\prime}(s)=0

i.e.
\lambda=s+\lambda_{0}
,
\mu=\mu_{0}
, a constant,
\dfrac{\tau}{\kappa}=\dfrac{s+\lambda_{0}}{\mu_{0}}

Thus
l^{2}=\lambda^{2}+\mu^{2}=s^{2}+2\lambda_{0}s+\left(\lambda_{0}^{2}+\mu_{0}^{2}\right)

i.e. distance function is a quadratic polynomial in arc length.

As
\dfrac{\tau}{\kappa}
is not a constant so it is not a generalized helix, rather a twisted curve having ratio, a linear function of are length. This is known as rectifying curve.

Also
\textit{{r}}\cdot\textit{{n}}=0
and
\textit{{r}}\cdot\textit{{b}}=\mu_{0}
, a constant; so the rectifying curve has constant normal component. Further
\textit{{r}}=\lambda\textit{{E}}+\mu~\textit{{b}}~\alpha~(\tau\textit{{t}}+\kappa\textit{{b}})=\textit{{d}}
, the Darboux vector. So, the position vector of a rectifying curve is always along the direction of the Darboux vector.

\bullet
Conditions for P.V. to be on normal plane:

Let
~~~\textit{{r}}(s)=\alpha(s)\textit{{n}}(s)+\beta(s)\textit{{b}}(s)

\therefore\textit{{t}}=\dfrac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}=\alpha^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\beta^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+\alpha\left(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}\right)-\beta\tau\textit{{n}}

\Rightarrow 1+\alpha\kappa=0,~\alpha\tau+\beta^{\prime}=0,~\alpha^{\prime}-\beta\tau=0

\Rightarrow\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}=
constant
=l_{0}^{2}

\therefore|\textit{{r}}|^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}=l_{0}^{2}

\Rightarrow
the curve is a spherical curve.

Suppose
\alpha=l_{0}\cos(m_{0}s)
,
\alpha=l_{0}\sin(m_{0}s)

then
\tau=-m_{0}
, a constant and
\kappa=-\dfrac{1}{l_{0}\cos(m_{0}s)}
.

As
\kappa
is not constant so it is not a circular curve, rather a spherical curve of constant torsion.

We now write the Serret-Frenet formulae i.e. eq. (
3.4
) in matrix form as

\frac{d}{ds}\begin{bmatrix}\textit{{t}}\\
\textit{{n}}\\
\textit{{b}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\kappa&0\\
-\kappa&0&\tau\\
0&-\tau&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\textit{{t}}\\
\textit{{n}}\\
\textit{{b}}\end{bmatrix}

Here
S=\begin{bmatrix}0&\kappa&0\\
-\kappa&0&\tau\\
0&-\tau&0\end{bmatrix}
is a skew-symmetric matrix and is termed as space matrix. The eigen values of this matrix are
0
,
\pm i\sqrt{\kappa^{2}+\tau^{2}}
with eigen vectors

\textit{{e}}_{\bm{0}}=\textit{{d}}
, the Darboux vector, and

\textit{{e}}_{\pm}=-\kappa\textit{{t}}\mp\textit{i}\sqrt{\kappa^{2}+\tau^{2}}\textit{{n}}+\tau\textit{{b}}

Note that
\textit{{e}}_{\bm{0}}
and
\textit{{e}}_{\pm}
are orthogonal to each other.

In general, the P.V. of a point on a space curve can be written as

\textit{{r}}(s)=u(s)\textit{{t}}+v(s)\textit{{n}}+w(s)\textit{{b}}

Now

V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{dt}=\frac{\partial\textit{{r}}}{\partial t}+\bm{\omega}\times\textit{{r}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\textit{{r}}}{\partial s}+\bm{\omega}\times\textit{{r}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\dot{u}-\kappa v)\textit{{t}}+(\dot{v}+\kappa u-\tau w)\textit{{n}}+(\dot{w}+\tau v)\textit{{b}}

(an overdot denotes differentiation with respect to ‘
s
’)

So

\bm{\omega}\times\textit{{r}}=-\kappa v\textit{{t}}+(\kappa u-\tau w)\textit{{n}}+\tau v\textit{{b}}

(3.13)

Suppose
~\bm{\omega}=\omega_{1}\textit{{t}}+\omega_{2}\textit{{n}}+\omega_{3}\textit{{b}}
, then

\displaystyle\bm{\omega}\times\textit{{r}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{vmatrix}\textit{{t}}&\textit{{n}}&\textit{{b}}\\
\omega_{1}&\omega_{2}&\omega_{3}\\
u&v&w\end{vmatrix}

(3.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(w\omega_{2}-v\omega_{3})\textit{{t}}+(u\omega_{3}-w\omega_{1})\textit{{n}}+(v\omega_{1}-u\omega_{2})\textit{{b}}

Thus comparing eqs. (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), one gets

w\omega_{2}-v\omega_{3}=-\kappa v,~u\omega_{3}-w\omega_{1}=\kappa u-\tau w
and
v\omega_{1}-u\omega_{2}=\tau v
.

Choosing
\omega_{2}=0,~\omega_{1}=\tau,\omega_{3}=\kappa
, the angular velocity
\bm{\omega}=\tau\textit{{t}}+\kappa\textit{{b}}=\textit{{d}}
i.e. rotation is along the Darboux vector.

|\vec{\omega}|=\sqrt{\tau^{2}+\kappa^{2}}
is the magnitude of the angular velocity. Thus Darboux vector is the instantaneous axis of rotation with magnitude of angular velocity
\sqrt{\tau^{2}+\kappa^{2}}
. Moreover, rectifying curves can be interpreted kinematically as those curves whose position vector field determines the axis of instantaneous rotation at each point of the curve.

Corollary II: If
\kappa_{n-1}=0
identically, then the equation for
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}}{ds}
(
i.e.
, eq. (
3.7
)) becomes

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-1}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{n-2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n-2}}

and the vector
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
is uncalled for. However,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
may be defined uniquely as a unit vector such that (
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}~,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
) form a right-handed orthonormal frame of the tangent space. Hence the Frenet frame is fully defined and the last equation (
i.e.
, eq. (
3.8
)) of Frenet formulae becomes

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}}{ds}=0~.

Next suppose
\kappa_{r-1}=0
identically for some
r<n
. Then the equation for
\dfrac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r-1}}}{ds}
becomes

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r-1}}}{ds}=-\kappa_{r-2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r-2}}

and the vector
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r}}
is undefined. Therefore, all subsequent
t
-vectors after
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r-1}}
are undefined. Also all curvatures after
\kappa_{r-2}
are undefined and may be treated to be all equal to zero. The curve is than said to be
(r-2)
-curvatured.

However, for
n
-dimensional Euclidean space, it can be proved that the curve lies in a
(r-1)
-plane. We may define vectors
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r}}~,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r+1}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
as constant unit vectors which are mutually orthogonal and orthogonal to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}~,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r-1}}
. In this case

\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r}}}{ds}=0~,~\ldots~,~\frac{\delta\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}}{ds}=0

and
\kappa_{r-1}=0=\kappa_{r}=\cdots=\kappa_{n}
identically. It may be mentioned that choice of the vectors
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{r}}~,\ldots,~\textit{{t}}_{\bm{n}}
are not at all unique.

3.5

Equations of a geodesic

Fig. 3.2

Suppose

\gamma:x^{i}=f^{i}(u)

be a curve with parameter
u
and let
P
,
Q
be two points on it with parametric values
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
respectively. Let
\gamma^{\prime}
be a neighbouring curve which also passes through the points
P
and
Q
as shown in the figure.

So the equation of
\gamma^{\prime}
can be written as

\overline{x}^{i}=x^{i}+\epsilon w^{i}~,

(3.15)

where
\epsilon
is a small scalar and
w^{i}
are functions of
x^{i}
(along the curve) such that

w^{i}=0
for
u=u_{0}
and
u=u_{1}
.

Now consider the integral

I=\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\Phi(x^{i},\dot{x}^{i})du}~,~~i=1,2,\ldots,n

where
\dot{x}^{i}=\dfrac{dx^{i}}{du}
and
\Phi
is an analytic function of the
2n
arguments
x^{i}
and
\dot{x}^{i}
. Then

\delta I=\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{i}}\delta x^{i}+\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\delta\dot{x}^{i}\right)du}+\cdots\cdots~,

where the dot terms are second and higher orders in the small quantities
\delta x^{i}
.

Now,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\delta\dot{x}^{i}du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\frac{d}{du}\left(\delta x^{i}\right)du}-\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\left[\left\{\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\right)\right\}\int{\delta\dot{x}^{i}}du\right]du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\delta x^{i}\right|_{P}^{Q}-\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\right)\delta x^{i}du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\right)\delta x^{i}du}~~~\left(\mbox{Since}~\delta x^{i}=\overline{x}^{i}-x^{i}=\epsilon w^{i}~,~\mbox{so}~\delta x^{i}=0~\mbox{at}~P~\mbox{and}~Q\right).

Therefore,

\delta I=\int\limits_{P}^{Q}{\left[\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\right)\right]\delta x^{i}du}~.

(3.16)

The integral
\delta I
is called the first variation of the integral
I
and
I
attains an extremal value on the curve
\gamma
in its immediate neighbourhood if
\delta I=0
for every set of functions
\delta x^{i}
(or
w^{i}
) vanishing on
P
and
Q
. A necessary and sufficient condition for this is

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{i}}\right)=0~,~~i=1,2,\ldots,n~,

(3.17)

which are known as Euler’s equations on the condition of extremality.

Now, the length of the curve
\gamma
from
P
to
Q
is

s=\int\limits_{u_{0}}^{u_{1}}{\sqrt{g_{\alpha\beta}\frac{dx^{\alpha}}{du}\frac{dx^{\beta}}{du}}du}=\int\limits_{u_{0}}^{u_{1}}{\sqrt{g_{\alpha\beta}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}}du}~.

The arc length
s
will be an extremal if (
3.17
) holds with

\phi=\sqrt{g_{\alpha\beta}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}}=\frac{ds}{du}~.

(3.18)

From geometric point of view, it is clear that
s
is an extremal will mean that
s
is a minimal.

Thus on differentiation,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{\mu}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{g_{\alpha\beta}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}}}\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}}{\frac{ds}{du}}

and

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{g_{\alpha\beta}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}}}2g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}=\frac{g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}}{\frac{ds}{du}}~.

So

\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial\dot{x}^{\mu}}\right)=\frac{\left\{\frac{ds}{du}\frac{d}{du}\left(g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\right)-g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\frac{d^{2}s}{du^{2}}\right\}}{\left(\frac{ds}{du}\right)^{2}}~.

Hence from eq. (
3.17
) ,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}-\frac{d}{du}\left(g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\right)+g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\frac{\frac{d^{2}s}{du^{2}}}{\left(\frac{ds}{du}\right)}=0

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}-\frac{\partial g_{\mu\beta}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}-g_{\mu\beta}\ddot{x}^{\beta}+g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\frac{\frac{d^{2}s}{du^{2}}}{\left(\frac{ds}{du}\right)}=0

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~g_{\mu\beta}\ddot{x}^{\beta}+\left(\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\beta}+\Gamma_{\alpha\beta\mu}\right)\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\beta}+\Gamma_{\mu\beta\alpha}\right)\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}-g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\left(\frac{\ddot{s}}{\dot{s}}\right)=0

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~g_{\mu\beta}\ddot{x}^{\beta}+\Gamma_{\alpha\beta\mu}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\beta}-g_{\mu\beta}\dot{x}^{\beta}\left(\frac{\ddot{s}}{\dot{s}}\right)=0~.

Transvecting by
g^{\delta\mu}
, we get

\frac{d^{2}x^{\delta}}{du^{2}}+\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\delta}\frac{dx^{\alpha}}{du}\frac{dx^{\beta}}{du}-\frac{dx^{\delta}}{du}\left(\frac{\ddot{s}}{\dot{s}}\right)=0.

(3.19)

This is the differential equation for a geodesic in
V_{n}
in terms of a general parameter
u
. However, if
u=s
, the arc length then
\dfrac{ds}{du}=1
and
\dfrac{d^{2}u}{ds^{2}}=0
. So the above geodesic equation simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}x^{\delta}}{ds^{2}}+\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\delta}\frac{dx^{\alpha}}{ds}\frac{dx^{\beta}}{ds}=0

(3.20)

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~\frac{\delta}{ds}\left(\frac{dx^{\delta}}{ds}\right)=0~.

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~\frac{\delta t^{\delta}}{ds}=0

Some consequences of geodesic equations:

(a) Definition : Parallel vectors along a curve: A vector field
V^{i}
defined along a curve
\gamma:x^{i}=f^{i}(s)
in
V_{n}
is said to be parallel along the curve or parallely transported along the curve if

\displaystyle\frac{\delta V^{i}}{ds}=0

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~\frac{dV^{i}}{ds}+\Gamma_{kl}^{i}V^{k}\frac{dx^{l}}{ds}=0.

Note: A curve is a geodesic if its tangent vector
t
is parallel along the curve. Hence a geodesic is also called an auto-parallel line.

(b) Theorem 3.1 : Any parallel vector field along a curve
\gamma
is of constant magnitude.

Proof: As
\left|A\right|^{2}=g_{ij}A^{i}A^{j}
, so

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\left|A\right|^{2}=\frac{d}{ds}(g_{ij}A^{i}A^{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{ds}(g_{ij}A^{i}A^{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta g_{ij}}{ds}A^{i}A^{j}+g_{ij}\frac{\delta A^{i}}{ds}A^{j}+g_{ij}A^{i}\frac{\delta A^{j}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~,

since
\dfrac{\delta g_{ij}}{ds}=0
, metric tensor is covariant constant and
\dfrac{\delta A^{i}}{ds}=0
,
A
is constant along the curve
\gamma
.

Hence the vector field
A
is of constant magnitude.

Note: As geodesic is an auto-parallel line,
i.e.
, tangent vector
t
is parallel along the geodesic, so tangent vector to the geodesic is of constant magnitude.

As
t^{i}=\dfrac{dx^{i}}{ds}
is the tangent vector to the geodesic, so from the geodesic equation (
3.20
), we have
\dfrac{\delta t^{i}}{ds}=0
. Now,

\displaystyle\left|\textit{{t}}\right|=g_{ij}\,t^{i}t^{j}=g_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

constant

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~~g_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{constant}~,

along the geodesic. This is called the first integral of the geodesic equation.

(c) Theorem 3.2 : If
A
and
B
be two vector fields parallel along the curve
\gamma
then
A
and
B
make a constant angle among them at every point on
\gamma
.

Proof:

\frac{d}{ds}(\textit{{A}}\textit{{B}})=\frac{d}{ds}(g_{ij}A^{i}B^{j})=\frac{\delta}{ds}(g_{ij}A^{i}B^{j})=0~~~(\mbox{Since}~\frac{\delta g_{ij}}{ds}=0=\frac{\delta A^{i}}{ds}=\frac{\delta B^{i}}{ds})~.

Hence the scalar product between the vectors
A
and
B
is a constant throughout the curve. Also the magnitudes of
A
and
B
are constants throughout the curve. So the angle
\theta
between the vectors
A
and
B
,
i.e.
,

\cos\theta=\frac{\textit{{A}}\cdot\textit{{B}}}{\left|\textit{{A}}\right|\left|\textit{{B}}\right|}

is also constant at every point of
\gamma
.

Note: If
v
is a vector field parallel along a geodesic then
v
is a vector field of constant magnitude and is at constant angle with the tangent field of the geodesic,
i.e.
, at constant angle with the geodesic.

(d) Definition : Geodesic coordinates: Usually, Cartesian coordinates are one for which co-efficients of first fundamental form (
i.e.

g_{ij}
) are constants. In general Riemannian space such a coordinate system is not possible throughout the space. However, it is possible to have a coordinate system such that
g_{ij}
are locally constants in the neighbourhood of a point
P\in M
,
i.e.
,

\frac{\delta g_{ij}}{\delta x^{k}}=0~~\mbox{at}~P~~~\mbox{and}~~~\frac{\delta g_{ij}}{\delta x^{k}}\neq 0~~\mbox{elsewhere}.

Then such a coordinate system is called a geodesic coordinate system with
P
as the pole. So it is evident that Christoffel’s symbols vanish at
P
and consequently the covariant derivative reduces to partial derivative at
P
.

Note: In geodesic coordinates, the geodesics at the pole
P
becomes identical to those in Euclidean geometry.

Theorem 3.3 : The necessary and sufficient conditions that a system of coordinates be geodesic with pole at
P
are that their second order covariant derivative with respect to the metric of the space all vanish at the pole
P
.

Proof: From the transformation law of Christoffel’s symbols, we have (see eq. (
2.41
))

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}_{lm}^{q}\frac{\partial x^{u}}{\partial\bar{x}^{q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\bar{x}^{l}}\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\bar{x}^{m}}\Gamma_{ik}^{u}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{u}}{\partial\bar{x}^{l}\partial\bar{x}^{m}}

\displaystyle\mbox{or},~~-\Gamma_{ik}^{u}\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial\bar{x}^{l}}\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\bar{x}^{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{x}^{m}}\left(\frac{\partial x^{u}}{\partial\bar{x}^{l}}\right)-\bar{\Gamma}_{lm}^{q}\frac{\partial x^{u}}{\partial\bar{x}^{q}}.

Now, interchanging bar and unbar coordinate system, we have

-\bar{\Gamma}_{ik}^{u}\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{i}}{\partial x^{l}}\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{k}}{\partial x^{m}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\left(\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{u}}{\partial x^{l}}\right)-\Gamma_{lm}^{q}\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{u}}{\partial x^{q}}=\nabla_{m}{\left(\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{u}}{\partial x^{l}}\right)}=\bar{x}^{u}_{;lm}~,

(3.21)

where we have assumed the bar coordinates as scalar functions of
{x^{i}}
’s. Now if we assume the bar coordinates as geodesic coordinates with
P
as the pole then
\bar{\Gamma}_{ik}^{u}=0
at
P
and hence from (
3.21
),

\bar{x}^{u}_{;lm}=0~~\mbox{at}~P~.

Conversely, if the second covariant derivatives vanish at
P
then from (
3.21
),

\bar{\Gamma}_{ik}^{u}\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{i}}{\partial x^{l}}\frac{\partial\bar{x}^{k}}{\partial x^{m}}=0~.

As
\dfrac{\partial\bar{x}^{i}}{\partial x^{l}}\neq 0
at
P
, so
\bar{\Gamma}_{ik}^{u}=0
at
P
,
i.e.
, the bar coordinate system is a geodesic coordinate with
P
as the pole.

Note: At the pole of the geodesic coordinate system, the first order covariant derivatives are ordinary partial derivatives while the second order covariant derivatives of the coordinate system with respect to the metric of the space vanishes identically.

3.6

Curves in three dimensions

Let a curve
\gamma
in three dimension is given by the parametric representation
x^{i}=f^{i}(u),~i=1,2,3
and
s
denotes the arc length along the curve
\gamma
. We shall start with the following theorem.

Theorem 3.4 : If the parametric representation of a curve is regular and of class
C^{k}
then the arc length
s
is a regular parameter of class
C^{k}
.

Proof: Let
P_{0}
(a fixed point) and
P
be two points on the curve having parameters
u_{0}
and
u
. Then the arc length from
P_{0}
to
P
is

s=\int\limits_{u_{0}}^{u}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{3}{f^{i}}^{\prime}(u){f^{i}}^{\prime}(u)}du}=\psi(u)~~~(\mbox{say}).

If
f^{1}(u)
,
f^{2}(u)
and
f^{3}(u)
are functions of class
C^{k}
then their derivatives are of class
C^{k-1}
. Hence when we take the integral we again get a function of class
C^{k}
. Thus
s=\psi(u)
is a function of class
C^{k}
. Hence the theorem.

Again,

\frac{ds}{du}=\sqrt{{\left\{{f^{1}}^{\prime}(u)\right\}}^{2}+{\left\{{f^{2}}^{\prime}(u)\right\}}^{2}+{\left\{{f^{3}}^{\prime}(u)\right\}}^{2}}\neq 0~.

Hence if
u
is a regular parameter then so is
s
. Also the correspondence between
s
and
u
is one-one and therefore
\psi
is invertible. So let us assume
u=\phi(s)
. Then
\phi(s)
is also a one-one function of class
C^{k}
. The curve is then given by

x^{i}=f^{i}(\phi(s))=g^{i}(s)

which is also a function of class
C^{k}
.

Tangent Vector: Let
x^{i}=x^{i}(u)
be a given curve on which
P=x^{i}(u)
and
Q=x^{i}(u+\Delta u)
be two neighbouring points. Then the limiting position of the vector
\dfrac{\textit{{PQ}}}{\Delta u}
as
Q\rightarrow P
,
i.e.
,
\Delta u\rightarrow 0
is a tangent vector to the curve at
P
. Now,

\lim_{\Delta u\rightarrow 0}\frac{\textit{{PQ}}}{\Delta u}=\lim_{\Delta u\rightarrow 0}\frac{x^{i}(u+\Delta u)-x^{i}(u))}{\Delta u}=\frac{dx^{i}}{du}~.

This is known as the parameter ruled forward tangent vector to the curve at
P
.

As

s=\int_{u_{0}}^{u}{\sqrt{\left(\frac{dx^{1}}{du}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{dx^{2}}{du}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{dx^{3}}{du}\right)^{2}}du}~,

so

\frac{ds}{du}=\sqrt{\left(\frac{dx^{1}}{du}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{dx^{2}}{du}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{dx^{3}}{du}\right)^{2}}~.

Thus putting
s=u
, we have

\left(\frac{dx^{1}}{ds}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{dx^{2}}{ds}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{dx^{3}}{ds}\right)^{2}=1~.

Hence
\dfrac{dx^{i}}{ds}
is the unit forward tangent vector to the curve or simply the tangent vector.

Example: The curve
x^{i}=(a\cos u,a\sin u,0)
represents a circle in the
x^{1}x^{2}
-plane. The tangent at the point
A(a,0,0)
is
\left.\dfrac{dx^{i}}{du}\right|_{u=0}=\left.(-a\sin u,a\cos u,0)\right|_{u=0}=(0,a,0)
.

3.7

Curves in a plane

Theorem 3.5 : A necessary and sufficient condition for the curve
x^{i}=f^{i}(u)
to lie on a plane is that

\begin{vmatrix}f^{1^{\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime}}\\
f^{1^{\prime\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime\prime}}\\
f^{1^{\prime\prime\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime\prime\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime\prime\prime}}\end{vmatrix}=0~~~~\forall u
.

Proof: First of all we shall prove that the condition is necessary. So we assume that
x^{i}=f^{i}(u)
be a plane curve and we shall have to show that the above determinant to be zero. Suppose the equation of the plane be

a_{1}x^{1}+a_{2}x^{2}+a_{3}x^{3}+a_{0}=0.

(3.22)

Then

a_{1}f^{1}(u)+a_{2}f^{2}(u)+a_{3}f^{3}(u)+a_{0}=0~~~~\forall u~.

By successive differentiation with respect to
u
, we get

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{c}a_{1}f^{1^{\prime}}(u)+a_{2}f^{2^{\prime}}(u)+a_{3}f^{3^{\prime}}(u)=0\\
a_{1}f^{1^{\prime\prime}}(u)+a_{2}f^{2^{\prime\prime}}(u)+a_{3}f^{3^{\prime\prime}}(u)=0\\
a_{1}f^{1^{\prime\prime\prime}}(u)+a_{2}f^{2^{\prime\prime\prime}}(u)+a_{3}f^{3^{\prime\prime\prime}}(u)=0~.\end{array}\right\}\forall u

As
a_{i}^{\prime}s
are not all equal to zero, hence eliminating
a_{i}^{\prime}s
we get

\begin{vmatrix}f^{1^{\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime}}\\
f^{1^{\prime\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime\prime}}\\
f^{1^{\prime\prime\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime\prime\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime\prime\prime}}\end{vmatrix}=0~~~~\forall u~.

So the condition is necessary.

To prove the condition sufficient, let us assume the condition. Then we know that for each value of
u
, numbers
b_{1}(u)
,
b_{2}(u)
and
b_{3}(u)
exist such that

\displaystyle b_{i}f^{i^{\prime}}~\equiv

\displaystyle b_{1}f^{1^{\prime}}+b_{2}f^{2^{\prime}}+b_{3}f^{3^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=0

(3.27)

\displaystyle b_{i}f^{i^{\prime\prime}}~\equiv

\displaystyle b_{1}f^{1^{\prime\prime}}+b_{2}f^{2^{\prime\prime}}+b_{3}f^{3^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=~0

(3.28)

\displaystyle b_{i}f^{i^{\prime\prime\prime}}~\equiv

\displaystyle b_{1}f^{1^{\prime\prime\prime}}+b_{2}f^{2^{\prime\prime\prime}}+b_{3}f^{3^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=~0~.

(3.29)

(Note that for a fixed
u
,
b_{1}(u)
,
b_{2}(u)
and
b_{3}(u)
are non-zero solutions of
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
in eq. (
3.22
)).

Now, differentiating eq. (
3.27
), we get

b_{i}f^{i^{\prime\prime}}+b_{i}^{\prime}f^{i^{\prime}}=0

which by virtue of eq. (
3.28
) gives

\displaystyle b_{i}^{\prime}f^{i^{\prime}}=0

(3.30)

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~b_{1}^{\prime}f^{1^{\prime}}+b_{2}^{\prime}f^{2^{\prime}}+b_{3}^{\prime}f^{3^{\prime}}=0~.

Similarly, differentiating eq. (
3.28
) and using eq. (
3.29
), we get

\displaystyle b_{i}^{\prime}f^{i^{\prime\prime}}=0

(3.31)

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~b_{1}^{\prime}f^{1^{\prime\prime}}+b_{2}^{\prime}f^{2^{\prime\prime}}+b_{3}^{\prime}f^{3^{\prime\prime}}=0~.

We now discuss the following two cases:

Case I: Let the rank of
\begin{bmatrix}f^{1^{\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime}}\\
f^{1^{\prime\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime\prime}}\end{bmatrix}
be two.

Then the solution space of the equations

\displaystyle xf^{1^{\prime}}+yf^{2^{\prime}}+zf^{3^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle xf^{1^{\prime\prime}}+yf^{2^{\prime\prime}}+zf^{3^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

in
x
,
y
,
z
is of rank 1. But
b_{i}
and
b_{i}^{\prime}
are both solutions and hence they must be proportional,
i.e.
,

\frac{b_{1}^{\prime}}{b_{1}}=\frac{b_{2}^{\prime}}{b_{2}}=\frac{b_{3}^{\prime}}{b_{3}}=\phi^{\prime}(u)~~~~(\mbox{say}).

Integrating with respect to
u
gives

\displaystyle\log b_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(u)+\log a_{i}

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~b_{i}~~

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{1}e^{\phi(u)}~~,~i=1,2,3.

Hence from eq. (
3.30
), we get (after cancelling the common factor
e^{\phi(u)}\phi^{\prime}(u)
)

\displaystyle a_{1}f^{1^{\prime}}+a_{2}f^{2^{\prime}}+a_{3}f^{3^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~a_{1}f^{1}+a_{2}f^{2}+a_{3}f^{3}+a_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~(\mbox{Integrating~once}).

Hence the curve is a plane curve lying on the plane

a_{1}x^{1}+a_{2}x^{2}+a_{3}x^{3}+a_{0}=0.

Case II: Let the rank of
\begin{bmatrix}f^{1^{\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime}}\\
f^{1^{\prime\prime}}&f^{2^{\prime\prime}}&f^{3^{\prime\prime}}\end{bmatrix}
be one.

Then
\dfrac{f^{1^{\prime\prime}}}{f^{1^{\prime}}}=\dfrac{f^{2^{\prime\prime}}}{f^{2^{\prime}}}=\dfrac{f^{3^{\prime\prime}}}{f^{3^{\prime}}}=\xi^{\prime}(u)
(say).

So on integration

\displaystyle\log f^{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi(u)+\lambda_{1}~~~({\lambda_{i}}\mbox{'s ~are ~constants})

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~f^{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{i}e^{\xi(u)}~~~(\mbox{writing}~a_{i}=e^{\lambda_{i}}).

Integrating once more, we get

f^{i}=a_{i}\psi(u)+b_{i}~~,~~\psi(u)=\int{e^{\xi(u)}du}

with
{b_{i}}
’s as integration constants.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\frac{f^{1}-b_{1}}{a_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{f^{2}-b_{2}}{a_{2}}=\frac{f^{3}-b_{3}}{a_{3}}

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~\frac{x^{1}-b_{1}}{a_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{x^{2}-b_{2}}{a_{2}}=\frac{x^{3}-b_{3}}{a_{3}}.

This shows that the curve is a straight line and therefore it is a plane curve.

Definition: A curve in
E_{3}
which is not a plane curve is called a twisted curve.

Example: A curve
x^{1}=au^{1}
,
x^{2}=bu^{2}
,
x^{3}=cu^{3}
(
a
,
b
,
c\neq 0
) is called a twisted curve as

\Delta=\begin{vmatrix}x^{1^{\prime}}&x^{2^{\prime}}&x^{3^{\prime}}\\
x^{1^{\prime\prime}}&x^{2^{\prime\prime}}&x^{3^{\prime\prime}}\\
x^{1^{\prime\prime\prime}}&x^{2^{\prime\prime\prime}}&x^{3^{\prime\prime\prime}}\end{vmatrix}=12abc\neq 0
.

Osculating plane: The osculating plane to a curve at a point
P
on it is the plane having the highest order
of contact with the curve at
P
.

Let
\gamma:x^{i}=x^{i}(u)
be the given curve. Any plane passing through
x^{i}
may be written as

\sum_{i}a_{i}(X^{i}-x^{i})=0~,

i.e.,~~~~a_{1}(X^{1}-x^{1})+a_{2}(X^{2}-x^{2})+a_{3}(X^{3}-x^{3})=0

(3.32)

where
X^{i}
is a general point on the plane.

The point
x^{i}(u+\epsilon)
will lie on the plane if

\sum a_{i}\left\{\epsilon x^{i^{\prime}}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2!}x^{i^{\prime\prime}}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\frac{\epsilon^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}x^{i^{(n-1)}}+\frac{\epsilon^{n}}{n!}x^{i^{(n)}}(u+\epsilon\theta)\right\}=0~~~(0<\theta<1).

(3.33)

This gives at least one root of
\epsilon
equal to zero implying that the plane passes through
P
. Let us choose
{a_{i}}^{{}^{\prime}}
s such that

\sum a_{i}x^{i^{\prime}}=0.

(3.34)

Then eq. (
3.33
) gives at least two roots of
\epsilon
equal to zero implying that the plane meets the curve at
P
in at least two contiguous points (
i.e.
, is of contact of order at least one). It may be noted that the plane now passes through the tangent at
P
.

Let
x^{i^{\prime\prime}}
be not proportional to
x^{i^{\prime}}
at
P
. We choose
{a_{i}}
’s such that we also have

\sum a_{i}x^{i^{\prime\prime}}=0.

(3.35)

Now eliminating
{a_{i}}
’s from equations (
3.32
), (
3.34
) and (
3.35
), we see that the equation of the plane is

\begin{vmatrix}X^{1}-x^{1}&X^{2}-x^{2}&X^{3}-x^{3}\\
x^{1^{\prime}}&x^{2^{\prime}}&x^{3^{\prime}}\\
x^{1^{\prime\prime}}&x^{2^{\prime\prime}}&x^{3^{\prime\prime}}\end{vmatrix}=0.

(3.36)

For this plane, eq. (
3.33
) gives at least three zero roots of
\epsilon
and the plane meets the curve at
P
in at least three contignous points and so the order of contact is at least two. The actual order of contact depends on the nature of the curve at
P
. If it happens that
x^{i(k)}
are linear combination of
x^{i^{\prime}}
’s and
x^{i^{\prime\prime}}
’s for
k=3,4,\ldots,p
but not for
k=(p+1)
then eq. (
3.33
) gives
(p+1)
zero roots of
\epsilon
(
(p+1)
contiguous points at
P
,
i.e.
, order of contact
p
). In this case no plane can have a contact of order higher than
p
because
a_{i}
would be required to satisfy

\sum a_{i}x^{i^{\prime}}=0~,~~~\sum a_{i}x^{i^{\prime\prime}}=0~,~~\cdots,~\sum a_{i}x^{i^{(p+1)}}=0~.

But
x^{i^{\prime}}~,~x^{i^{\prime\prime}}~,\ldots,~x^{i^{(p+1)}}
being independent vectors, implies
a_{1}=a_{2}=\cdots=0
and the plane is undefined. Thus eq. (
3.36
) gives the osculating plane for the curve.

3.8

The moving trihedron (Frenet frame)

The osculating plane at a point
P
is spanned by the vectors
\dfrac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}
and
\dfrac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{du^{2}}
, where
u
is the parameter of the curve (the two vectors are assumed to be independent). As this being true for any parameter so it is also true for arc length
s
. Let us write
\textit{{t}}=\dfrac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}
, then
\dfrac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}=\dfrac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}
and the osculating plane is spanned by these two independent vectors
t
and
\dfrac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}
. Since
t
is the unit tangent vector so

\textit{{t}}\cdot\textit{{t}}=1~.

Thus on differentiation

\textit{{t}}\cdot\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}=0~.

Thus
t
and
\dfrac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}
are independent, non-zero vectors perpendicular to each other and lie on the osculating plane. The unit vector
\textit{{n}}=\dfrac{\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}}{\left|\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}\right|}
is called the principal normal vector. Suppose

\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}=\kappa\textit{{n}}~~\mbox{with}~~\kappa=\left|\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}\right|=\left|\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}\right|~,

where
\kappa
is called the first curvature or simply the curvature of the curve and is positive definite. We now define a vector
b
by the relation

\textit{{b}}=\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{n}}~.

Then
b
is perpendicular to
t
and is thus a normal vector. It is called the binormal vector. The triad of vectors
\{\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}},\textit{{b}}\}
forms a right handed orthonormal frame, called Frenet frame at the point
P
. As
P
moves along the curve, we call the variable frame
\{\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}},\textit{{b}}\}
the moving Frenet frame or moving trihedron.

Note: Let
v
be a unit vector field defined along a curve, then
\left|\dfrac{d\textit{{v}}}{ds}\right|
gives the arc rate of turning of the vector
v
.

3.8.1

Serret
-
Frenet Formulae

The formulae expressing
\textit{{t}}^{\prime}
,
\textit{{n}}^{\prime}
,
\textit{{b}}^{\prime}
(
{}^{\prime}\equiv\dfrac{d}{ds}
) as linear combination of (
t
,
n
,
b
) constitute what are called Serret-Frenet formulae or simply Frenet formulae. For convenience, let us consider any moving orthogonal frame
\{\textit{{e}}_{\bm{1}},\textit{{e}}_{\bm{2}},\textit{{e}}_{\bm{3}}\}
of vectors moving along the curve. We put

\textit{{e}}^{\prime}_{\bm{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}a_{ij}\textit{{e}}_{\bm{j}}~.

(3.37)

We show that the matrix
A=[a_{ij}]
called the Cartan matrix of the frame
\{\textit{{e}}_{\bm{i}}\}
is skew-symmetric. From eq. (
3.37
), taking scalar product with
\textit{{e}}_{\bm{k}}
, we get

\textit{{e}}^{\prime}_{\bm{i}}\cdot\textit{{e}}_{\bm{k}}=\sum_{j}a_{ij}\textit{{e}}_{\bm{j}}\textit{{e}}_{\bm{k}}=\sum_{j}a_{ij}\delta_{jk}=a_{ik}~.

Again from the relation

\textit{{e}}_{\bm{i}}\cdot\textit{{e}}_{\bm{k}}=\delta_{ik}~,

we get

\textit{{e}}^{\prime}_{\bm{i}}\cdot\textit{{e}}_{\bm{k}}+\textit{{e}}_{\bm{i}}\cdot\textit{{e}}^{\prime}_{\bm{k}}=0~,~~~i.e.,~~a_{ik}+a_{ki}=0.

Hence the Cartan matrix
[a_{ij}]
is a skew-symmetric matrix. If we now consider vectors
t
,
n
,
b
for
\textit{{e}}_{\bm{1}}
,
\textit{{e}}_{\bm{2}}
,
\textit{{e}}_{\bm{3}}
respectively and noting that we already have

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa\textit{{n}}~,

we get
a_{12}=\kappa
,
a_{13}=0
,
a_{21}=-\kappa
,
a_{31}=0
.

Also, if we put
a_{23}=\tau
and call it the second curvature or the torsion of the curve then we have
a_{32}=-\tau
and we obtain the Frenet formulae

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa\textit{{n}}~~,~~\textit{{n}}^{\prime}=-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}~~,~~\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau\textit{{n}}~.

From the third formula
\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau\textit{{n}}
, we get
\left|\textit{{b}}^{\prime}\right|=\left|\tau\right|
. As
b
is a unit vector so
\textit{{b}}^{\prime}
gives the arc-rate of turning of the binormal. Its magnitude is equal to the magnitude of torsion. Thus within sign the torsion
\tau
gives the arc-rate of turning of the binormal.
\tau
may be positive, negative or zero.

A curve in
E^{3}
is called a twisted curve if it has non-zero curvature and torsion.

Determination of
t
,
n
,
b
,
\kappa
,
\tau
:

For any curve
\textit{{r}}=\textit{{r}}(s)
, we have

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{\prime}=\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

t

(3.38)

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\textit{{n}}

(3.39)

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\frac{d^{3}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa\frac{d\textit{{n}}}{ds}=\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})

(3.40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa^{2}\textit{{t}}+\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa\tau\textit{{b}}~.

Eq. (
3.38
) gives
\textit{{t}}=\textit{{r}}^{\prime}
.

Eq. (
3.39
) gives
\kappa=\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|
(Since
\kappa
is non-negative).

Therefore,

\textit{{n}}=\frac{\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}}{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|}~.

From equations (
3.38
), (
3.39
) and (
3.40
), we get

\displaystyle[\textit{{r}}^{\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\textit{{t}},\kappa\textit{{n}},-\kappa^{2}\textit{{t}}+\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa\tau\textit{{b}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\tau[\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}},\textit{{b}}]=\kappa^{2}\tau

Therefore

\tau=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}[\textit{{r}}^{\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}]=\frac{[\textit{{r}}^{\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}]}{|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}}.

(3.41)

Also

\textit{{b}}=\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{n}}=\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\frac{\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}}{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|}=\frac{\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}}{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|}~.

(3.42)

Again from equations (
3.38
) and (
3.39
), we have

\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}=\textit{{t}}\times\kappa\textit{{n}}=\kappa\textit{{b}}

i.e.,~~~~\textit{{b}}=\frac{\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}}{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|}

(3.43)

\mbox{and}~~~~\kappa=\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|~.

Therefore, we can write

\tau=\frac{\left[\textit{{r}}^{\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]}{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|^{2}}~.

(3.44)

However, if the equation of the curve is given in parametric form with parameter
u
(say) (different from
s
),
i.e.
,

\textit{{r}}=\textit{{r}}(u)

then

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}\frac{ds}{du}

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{du^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}\left(\frac{ds}{du}\right)^{2}+\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}\frac{d^{2}s}{du^{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{3}}\left(\frac{ds}{du}\right)^{3}+3\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}\frac{ds}{du}\frac{d^{2}s}{du^{2}}+\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}\frac{d^{3}s}{du^{3}},

or in compact notation (
\cdot

\equiv

\dfrac{d}{du}
,
′

\equiv

\dfrac{d}{ds}
)

\displaystyle\dot{\textit{{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\dot{s}

(3.45)

\displaystyle\ddot{\textit{{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\dot{s}^{2}+\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\ddot{s}

\displaystyle\ddot{\textit{{r}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\dot{s}^{3}+3\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\dot{s}\ddot{s}+\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\,\dddot{s}~.

If we assume the arc length
s
and the parameter
u
in the same direction,
i.e.
,
\dfrac{ds}{du}=\dot{s}>0
then we have from eq. (
3.45
),

\dot{s}=\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\right|

(3.46)

\textit{{t}}=\frac{\dot{\textit{{r}}}}{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\right|}~.

(3.47)

Now

\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}=(\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime})\dot{s}^{3}=(\textit{{t}}\times\kappa\textit{{n}})\dot{s}^{3}=\kappa\dot{s}^{3}\textit{{b}}~.

As
\kappa
and
\dot{s}
are both positive so the unit vector
b
is given by

\textit{{b}}=\frac{\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}}{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|}~.

(3.48)

Also

\kappa\dot{s}^{3}=\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|~.

Therefore

\kappa=\frac{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|}{\dot{s}^{3}}=\frac{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|}{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{3}}~.

(3.49)

From equation (
3.45
) , we get

\left[\dot{\textit{{r}}},\ddot{\textit{{r}}},\dddot{\textit{{r}}}\right]=\left[\textit{{r}}^{\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]\dot{s}^{6}=\kappa^{2}\tau\dot{s}^{6}~.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}\dot{s}^{6}}\left[\dot{\textit{{r}}},\ddot{\textit{{r}}},\dddot{\textit{{r}}}\right]

(3.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{6}}{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}}\frac{\left[\dot{\textit{{r}}},\ddot{\textit{{r}}},\dddot{\textit{{r}}}\right]}{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{6}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left[\dot{\textit{{r}}},\ddot{\textit{{r}}},\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right]}{\left|\dot{\textit{{r}}}\times\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}}~.

Problem 3.1. Show that
\kappa=\dfrac{\sqrt{\left|\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}-\ddot{s}^{2}}}{\dot{s}^{2}}
.

3.9

Cylindrical Helix

A cylindrical helix is a twisted curve lying on a cylinder and meeting all generators at a constant angle. The curve is often simply called a helix.

Let
x^{3}
-axis be parallel to the generators. We first take the equation of the helix in the parametric form as

x^{1}=f^{1}(u)~,~~~x^{2}=f^{2}(u)~,~~~x^{3}=f^{3}(u)~.

As it cuts the generators at a constant angle
\alpha
so taking
\textit{{m}}=(0,0,1)
, we get

\cos\alpha=\frac{\textit{{m}}\cdot\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}}{\left|\textit{{m}}\right|\cdot\left|\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}\right|}=\frac{f^{3^{\prime}}(u)}{\sqrt{{\left\{f^{1^{\prime}}(u)\right\}}^{2}+{\left\{f^{2^{\prime}}(u)\right\}}^{2}+{\left\{f^{3^{\prime}}(u)\right\}}^{2}}}~.

Fig. 3.3

Hence

f^{3^{\prime}}(u)=\cot\alpha\sqrt{\left(f^{1^{\prime}}\right)^{2}+\left(f^{2^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}~,

i.e.~~~~f^{3}(u)=\cot\alpha\int{\sqrt{\left(f^{1^{\prime}}\right)^{2}+\left(f^{2^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}}du~.

So the equation of the cylindrical helix can be written in the form

x^{1}=f^{1}(u)~,~~~x^{2}=f^{2}(u)~,~~~x^{3}=\cot\alpha\int{\sqrt{\left(f^{1^{\prime}}\right)^{2}+\left(f^{2^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}}du~.

(3.51)

Note :
\alpha\neq\frac{\pi}{2}
, otherwise the curve will be a plane curve in a normal section to the cylinder.

If the cylinder is a right circular cylinder then the helix is called a circular helix. So, if the circular cylinder is given by

x^{1}=a\cos u~,~~~x^{2}=a\sin u~,

then the circular helix has the equation

x^{1}=a\cos u~,~~~x^{2}=a\sin u~,~~~x^{3}=a\cot\alpha u~,

where
a
(
\neq 0
) ,
\alpha\left(\neq\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)
are constants. Now putting
b=a\cot\alpha
, the equation of the circular helix may be written as

x^{1}=a\cos u~,~~~x^{2}=a\sin u~,~~~x^{3}=bu~.

(3.52)

We shall now determine the tangent vector (
t
) , the principal normal vector (
n
) , the binormal vector (
b
) , the curvature scalar (
\kappa
) and the torsion (
\tau
) for a circular helix :

For a circular helix,

\textit{{r}}=(a\cos u,a\sin u,bu)~.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-a\sin u,a\cos u,b)~,

t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}}{\left|\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}\right|}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}(-a\sin u,a\cos u,b)~.

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{ds}{du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}\bigg|=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}(-a\sin u,a\cos u,b)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}(-a\cos u,-a\sin u,0)\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a^{2}+b^{2}}(-a\cos u,-a\sin u,0)~.

Hence

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}\right|=\frac{a}{a^{2}+b^{2}}~,~~a~\mbox{is positive}

n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}}{\kappa}=(-\cos u,-\sin u,0)

b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{n}}=(b\sin u,-b\cos u,a)\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}~.

Also

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{du^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-a\cos u,-a\sin u,0)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}\textit{{r}}}{du^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a\sin u,-a\cos u,0)

Therefore

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}s^{6}}\left[\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}~~~~\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{du^{2}}~~~~\frac{d^{3}\textit{{r}}}{du^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(a^{2}+b^{2}\right)^{2}}{a^{2}}\frac{1}{\left(a^{2}+b^{2}\right)^{3}}\begin{vmatrix}-a\sin u&a\cos u&b\\
-a\cos u&-a\sin u&0\\
a\sin u&-a\cos u&0\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a^{2}+b^{2}}~.

Thus
\kappa
and
\tau
are both constants for a circular helix.

Note: In general,
\kappa
and
\tau
are given functions of the parameter of the curve. However, if
\kappa
and
\tau
are both constants then the curve is a circular helix.

Properties of a general cylindrical helix:

Let
m
denotes the unit vector along the generator of the cylinder and
\alpha
be the constant angle which the tangent vector makes with
m
,
i.e.
, the helix
H
is characterized by the relation

\textit{{t}}\cdot\textit{{m}}=\cos\alpha~,~~\mbox{a constant.}

(3.53)

Now differentiating with respect to the arc length
s
, we get

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{m}}=0,~~~i.e.,~~\textit{{n}}\cdot\textit{{m}}=0~.

(3.54)

Thus the principal normal is always perpendicular to the generators of the cylinder in which the helix lies. So the generator lies in the plane of
t
and
b
. Further, as
m
makes an angle
\alpha
with
t
, so we can assume that it is so directed that it makes an angle
\dfrac{\pi}{2}-\alpha
with
b
. Then

\textit{{b}}\cdot\textit{{m}}=\sin\alpha~.

(3.55)

Again differentiating eq. (
3.54
) with respect to
s
, we have

\displaystyle\textit{{n}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{m}}=0~,~~i.e.,~~(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~-\kappa\cos\alpha+\tau\sin\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~~\frac{\kappa}{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tan\alpha~,~~\mbox{a constant.}

(3.56)

Thus curvature and torsion are in a constant ratio.

Thus a general cylindrical helix has the following properties:

(a) The tangent to the cylindrical helix makes a constant angle with a fixed direction, chosen as the generator of the cylinder.

(b) The principal normal to the cylindrical helix is orthogonal to the fixed direction,
i.e.
, perpendicular to the generator. In fact, the generator of the cylinder lies in the plane of
t
and
b
.

(c) The curvature and torsion bears a constant ratio at every point of the helix.

It is to be noted that any one of the above three conditions is sufficient for a twisted curve to be a cylindrical helix.
Now we shall show that any one of the above three conditions is sufficient for a twisted curve to be a cylindrical helix,
i.e.
, eq. (
3.53
) is satisfied.

Suppose for a twisted curve

\displaystyle\textit{{n}}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\textit{{t}}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{constant}.

Hence by eq. (
3.53
) the curve is a cylindrical helix.

Suppose for a twisted curve

\displaystyle\textit{{b}}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

constant

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\textit{{b}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\textit{{n}}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\textit{{t}}\cdot\textit{{m}}

\displaystyle=

constant,

i.e.
, the curve is a cylindrical helix.

Suppose for a twisted curve

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{\tau}=\rho~,~~\mbox{a constant}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\kappa-\rho\tau=0.

By Frenet formulae, we have

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa\textit{{n}}~~~\mbox{and}~~~\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau\textit{{n}}~.

Therefore,

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}+\rho\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=0~~,~~i.e.,~~~\textit{{t}}+\rho\textit{{b}}=\textit{{l}}~,~~\mbox{a constant vector.}

Taking scalar product with
t
, we get

\textit{{l}}\cdot\textit{{t}}=1~.

Thus
\textit{{m}}=\dfrac{\textit{{l}}}{\left|\textit{{l}}\right|}
is a constant unit vector such that

\textit{{m}}\cdot\textit{{t}}=\mbox{constant.}

Hence the twisted curve is a cylindrical helix.

Note:
R=\dfrac{1}{\kappa}
is called the radius of curvature and
T=\dfrac{1}{\tau}
is called the radius of torsion.
