———————————————————————————–

Exercise

3.1. Show that
\kappa=\dfrac{\sqrt{\left|\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}-\ddot{s}^{2}}}{\dot{s}^{2}}
.

3.2. Show that for a circular helix, the principal normal is perpendicular to the generators of the cylinder.

3.3. Show that the arc length of a circular helix bears a constant ratio to that of its projection on the plane normal to the generator of the cylinder.

3.4. For the curve
\textit{{r}}=(3u,3u^{2},2u^{3})
, show that

R=T=\frac{3}{2}(1+2u^{2})^{2}~.

3.5. For the curve
\textit{{r}}=\left(u,\dfrac{u^{2}}{2\alpha},\dfrac{u^{3}}{6\alpha^{2}}\right)
, show that

\kappa=\tau=\frac{4\alpha^{3}}{(u^{2}+2\alpha^{2})^{2}}~.

3.6. Show that for the curve
\textit{{r}}=(u^{3}+3u,3u^{2},u^{3}-3u)
, the curvature and the torsion are equal.

3.7. Show that (a)
\tau\textit{{t}}^{\prime}+\kappa\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=0
;
\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\kappa\tau
.

(b)
\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime}=\tau(\kappa\textit{{t}}-\tau\textit{{b}})-\tau^{\prime}\textit{{n}}
.

(c)
\textit{{n}}^{\prime\prime}=\tau^{\prime}\textit{{b}}-(\kappa^{2}+\tau^{2})\textit{{n}}-\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{t}}
.

3.8. Show that
\dfrac{\left[\textit{{t}}^{\prime},\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]}{\left[\textit{{b}}^{\prime},\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]}=-\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{\tau}\right)^{3}
.

3.9. Show that
\left[T(R\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}\right]^{\prime}+\left[\dfrac{T}{R}\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\right]^{\prime}+\dfrac{R}{T}\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}=0
.

3.10. Find
t
,
n
,
b
,
\kappa
,
\tau
for the curve
\textit{{r}}=
(
a\cos u
,
b\sin u
,
0
) , where
a
,
b
are positive constants.

3.11. Show that a necessary and sufficient condition for a curve to be a straight line is
\kappa=0
.

3.12. Show that a necessary and sufficient condition for a curve to be a plane curve is
\tau=0
.

3.13. Determine
f(u)
so that the curve

x^{1}=a\cos u~,~~~x^{2}=a\sin u~,~~~x^{3}=f(u)

is a plane curve and find the nature of the curve.

3.14. Show that the torsion may be evaluated by the formula

\tau^{2}=\frac{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right|^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}-\kappa^{2}-\left(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\kappa}\right)^{2}~.

3.15. Show that for a curve with non-vanishing curvature and torsion, the condition

\frac{R}{T}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}=0

is equivalent to

\kappa^{3}\tau^{3}-\kappa^{2}\kappa^{\prime\prime}\tau+2\kappa{\kappa^{\prime}}^{2}\tau+\kappa^{2}\kappa^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}=0~.

3.16. Prove that the principal normal to the locus of the centre of spherical curvature is parallel to the principal normal to the original curve at the corresponding point.

3.17. Find the necessary and sufficient condition that a curve is a spherical curve.

3.18. If
M(\textit{{r}})
describes a twisted curve of constant curvature and
M(\overline{\textit{{r}}})
describes the locus of its centre of circular curvature then show that each curve is the locus of circular curvature of the other. Show also that they have the same curvature and this common curvature is the geometric mean of their torsions.

3.19. Show that if the principal normals of a curve be binormals of another then the curvature and torsion of the given curve must satisfy the relation

c(\kappa^{2}+\tau^{2})=\kappa~~,~~\mbox{for some cosntant}~c~.

3.20. If all the osculating planes of a curve have a point in common then the curve is a plane curve.

3.21. If the
n^{th}
derivative of
r
with respect to ‘
s
’ is given by the recurrence relation:

\textit{{r}}^{\bm{(n)}}=a_{n}\textit{{t}}+b_{n}\textit{{n}}+c_{n}\textit{{b}}

then prove the following relations:

i)
a_{n+1}=a_{n}^{\prime}-\kappa b_{n}
,

ii)
b_{n+1}=b_{n}^{\prime}+\kappa a_{n}-\tau c_{n}
,

and iii)
c_{n+1}=c_{n}^{\prime}+\tau b_{n}
.

3.22. If the tangent and the binormal at a point of a curve make angles
\theta
and
\phi
respectively with a fixed direction then show that

\frac{\sin\theta\,d\theta}{\sin\phi\,d\phi}=-\frac{\kappa}{\tau}~.

3.23. Prove that principal normals at consecutive points do not intersect unless
\tau=0
.

3.24. Prove that the position vector of the current point on a curve satisfies the differential equation:

\frac{d}{ds}\left\{T\frac{d}{ds}\left(R\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}\right)\right\}+\frac{d}{ds}\left(\frac{T}{R}\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}\right)+\frac{R}{T}\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{ds^{2}}=0~.

3.25. If
\bar{s}
be the arc length of the locus of the centre of curvature, then show that

\left(\frac{d\bar{s}}{ds}\right)=\sqrt{(R^{\prime})^{2}+\left(\frac{R}{T}\right)^{2}}~.

Solution and Hints

Solution 3.1:
\dot{\textit{{r}}}=\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\dot{s}~,~~\ddot{\textit{{r}}}=\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\dot{s}^{2}+\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\ddot{s}

Therefore

\displaystyle\left|\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|^{2}\dot{s}^{4}+\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\right|^{2}\ddot{s}^{2}~~(\mbox{since}~~\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}=\textit{{t}}\cdot\kappa\textit{{n}}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\dot{s}^{4}+\ddot{s}^{2}~.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\dot{s}^{4}+\ddot{s}^{2}

Thus
\kappa^{2}=\dfrac{\left|\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}-\ddot{s}^{2}}{\dot{s}^{4}}

Hence
\kappa=\dfrac{\sqrt{\left|\ddot{\textit{{r}}}\right|^{2}-\ddot{s}^{2}}}{\dot{s}^{2}}

Solution 3.2: For a circular helix,

r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a\cos u,a\sin u,bu)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{d^{2}\textit{{r}}}{du^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-a\cos u,-a\sin u,0)~.

So the principal normal
n
is along (
-\cos u,-\sin u,0
). The generator of the cylinder is along (0, 0, 1). Hence
n
is orthogonal to the generators of the cylinder.

Solution 3.3: Let the circular helix be given by

H:~\textit{{r}}=(a\cos u,a\sin u,bu)~.

So

\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}=(-a\sin u,a\cos u,b)~,~~i.e.,~~\left|\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}\right|=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}~.

Therefore,

t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}=\frac{\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}}{|\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{du}|}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}(-a\sin u,a\cos u,b)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{ds}{du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}~.

Now, a typical plane normal to the generator of the cylinder is the
x^{1}x^{2}
-plane. So the projection of
H
on
x^{1}x^{2}
-plane is the curve

H^{*}:\textit{{r}}^{\bm{*}}=(a\cos u,a\sin u,0)~.

If
s^{*}
denote the arc length of this curve
H^{*}
then from the above we have

\displaystyle\frac{ds^{*}}{du}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{ds^{*}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{ds^{*}}{du}}{\frac{ds}{du}}=\frac{a}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}~,~~\mbox{a constant.}

Hence the result.

Solution 3.7: (a) By Frenet’s formula

\textit{{t}}=\kappa\textit{{n}}~~~\mbox{and}~~~\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau\textit{{n}}~.

Therefore,

\tau\textit{{t}}^{\prime}+\kappa\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=0~.

Also,

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=\kappa\textit{{n}}(-\tau\textit{{n}})=-\kappa\tau~.

(b)
\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle\therefore~~~~\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tau\textit{{n}}^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime}\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tau(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})-\tau^{\prime}\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(\kappa\textit{{t}}-\tau\textit{{b}})-\tau^{\prime}\textit{{n}}~.

(c) We have
\textit{{n}}^{\prime}=-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}
.

\displaystyle\therefore~~~~\textit{{n}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{t}}-\kappa\textit{{t}}^{\prime}+\tau^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+\tau\textit{{b}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{t}}-\kappa(\kappa\textit{{n}})+\tau^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+\tau(-\tau\textit{{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}\textit{{b}}-(\kappa^{2}+\tau^{2})\textit{{n}}-\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{b}}~.

Solution 3.8:

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa\textit{{n}}^{\prime}=\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})=-\kappa^{2}\textit{{t}}+\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa\tau\textit{{b}}

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\kappa\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{t}}-\left(\kappa^{3}+\kappa\tau^{2}-\kappa^{\prime\prime}\right)\textit{{n}}+(2\kappa^{\prime}\tau+\kappa\tau^{\prime})\textit{{b}}~.

Then,

\left[\textit{{t}}^{\prime},\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]=-\kappa^{3}(\kappa^{\prime}\tau-\kappa\tau^{\prime})~.

Similarly,

\left[\textit{{b}}^{\prime},\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]=\tau^{3}(\kappa^{\prime}\tau-\kappa\tau^{\prime})~.

Thus,

\frac{\left[\textit{{t}}^{\prime},\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]}{\left[\textit{{b}}^{\prime},\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{b}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]}=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\right)^{3}~,~~\mbox{provided}~~\kappa^{\prime}\tau-\kappa\tau^{\prime}\neq 0~.

Note: If
\kappa^{\prime}\tau-\kappa\tau^{\prime}=0~
then
\dfrac{\kappa}{\tau}=
constant and the curve is a helix. Both the scalar triple product vanish identically.

Solution 3.9:

\displaystyle LHS

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{\tau}\left\{\frac{1}{\kappa}(\kappa\textit{{n}})\right\}^{\prime}\right]^{\prime}+\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\textit{{t}}\right]^{\prime}+\frac{\tau}{\kappa}(\kappa\textit{{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{\tau}\textit{{n'}}\right]^{\prime}+\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\textit{{t}}\right]^{\prime}+\tau\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{\tau}(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})\right]^{\prime}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\right)^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\textit{{t'}}+\tau\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\textit{{t}}\right)^{\prime}+\textit{{b'}}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\right)^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\tau}\textit{{n}}+\tau\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\right)^{\prime}\textit{{t}}-\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\kappa\textit{{n}}-\tau\textit{{n}}+\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\right)^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\tau}\textit{{n}}+\tau\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~.

Solution 3.11: Let the curve be

x^{1}=x^{1}(u)~,~~~x^{2}=x^{2}(u)~,~~~x^{3}=x^{3}(u)~.

We know that

\kappa=\frac{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|}{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\right|^{3}}~~~(\,^{\prime}~\mbox{stands for differentiation with respect to}~u).

So,

\kappa=0~~~\Rightarrow~~\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|=0~~~\Rightarrow~~\textit{{r}}^{\prime}\times\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}=0

\Rightarrow~~\frac{{x^{1}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{1}}^{\prime}}=\frac{{x^{2}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{2}}^{\prime}}=\frac{{x^{3}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{3}}^{\prime}}=\xi^{\prime}(u)~~~(\mbox{say}).

(3.57)

We shall prove that eq. (
3.57
) is also a necessary and sufficient condition for the curve to be a straight line.

Suppose equation (
3.57
) holds. So on integration, we have

{x^{i}}^{\prime}=a^{i}e^{\xi(u)}~,~~~i=1,2,3~.

Integrating once more,

x^{i}=a^{1}\psi(u)+b^{i}~,~~~\psi(u)=\int{e^{f(u)}du}~.

Thus we have

\frac{x^{1}-b^{1}}{a^{1}}=\frac{x^{2}-b^{2}}{a^{2}}=\frac{x^{3}-b^{3}}{a^{3}}~,

i.e.
, the curve is a straight line.

Conversely, let the curve be a straight line. Then its equation can be written as

\frac{x^{1}-b^{1}}{a^{1}}=\frac{x^{2}-b^{2}}{a^{2}}=\frac{x^{3}-b^{3}}{a^{3}}=\psi(u)~~(\mbox{say}),

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~~x^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{i}\psi(u)+b^{i}~,~~~i=1,2,3

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~{x^{i}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{i}\psi^{\prime}(u)~,~~~{x^{i}}^{\prime\prime}=a^{i}\psi^{\prime\prime}(u)

Therefore,

\frac{{x^{i}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{i}}^{\prime}}=\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(u)}{\psi^{\prime}(u)}=\xi(u)~~(\mbox{say}),

i.e.,~~~\frac{{x^{1}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{1}}^{\prime}}=\frac{{x^{2}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{2}}^{\prime}}=\frac{{x^{3}}^{\prime\prime}}{{x^{3}}^{\prime}}~,

which is relation (
3.57
).

Hence
\kappa=0
is both necessary and sufficient condition for a curve to be a straight line.

Solution 3.12: Let the curve be a plane curve. Then the osculating planes are constant, being the same as the plane of the curve. The binormal vector
b
is therefore a constant vector. Hence

\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=0~,~~~i.e.,~~-\tau\textit{{n}}=0~,~~~i.e.,~~\tau=0~.

Conversely, let
\tau=0
, then
\textit{{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau\textit{{n}}=0
. So
b
is a constant vector. Let

\displaystyle b^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{i}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~\frac{d}{ds}\left(\sum b^{i}x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma{b^{i}}^{\prime}x^{i}+\Sigma b^{i}t^{i}=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~~~~\sum b^{i}x^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{constant}=C

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~x^{1}\lambda^{1}+x^{2}\lambda^{2}+x^{3}\lambda^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C~,~~\mbox{a plane.}

Hence the curve is a plane curve.

Solution 3.13: The curve lies on the circular cylinder

x^{1}=a\cos u~,~~~x^{2}=a\sin u~.

(3.58)

Since the projection of the curve in the
x^{1}x^{2}
-plane is the circle given by eq. (
3.58
) so if the curve has to be a plane curve, it must be a plane section of the circular cylinder by a plane not parallel to the
x^{3}
-axis,
i.e.
, the axis of the cylinder.

Now the necessary and sufficient condition that the curve is a plane curve is

\displaystyle\left[\textit{{r}}^{\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime},\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right]=0~,

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~{\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}-a\sin u&a\cos u&f^{\prime}(u)\\
-a\cos u&-a\sin u&f^{\prime\prime}(u)\\
a\sin u&-a\cos u&f^{\prime\prime\prime}(u)\end{array}\right|}=0~,

\displaystyle i.e.,

\displaystyle~f^{\prime\prime\prime}(u)+f^{\prime}(u)=0.

Hence the solution for
f(u)
is

f(u)=a_{1}\cos u+b_{1}\sin u+c_{1}~,

where
a_{1}
,
b_{1}
and
c_{1}
are arbitrary constants.

Solution 3.14:

t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}=\textit{{r}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{t}}}{ds}=\textit{{t'}}=\kappa\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa\textit{{n'}}=\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\kappa(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa^{4}+(\kappa^{\prime})^{2}+\kappa^{2}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~\tau^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\textit{{r}}^{\prime\prime}\right|^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}-\kappa^{2}-\left(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\kappa}\right)^{2}~.

Solution 3.15: The condition
\dfrac{R}{T}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}=0
is equivalent to
\dfrac{\tau}{\kappa}+\left(-\dfrac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\kappa^{2}\tau}\right)^{\prime}=0
and this gives the result.

Solution 3.16: Let
C(\textit{{r}})
and
\overline{C}(\overline{\textit{{r}}})
be the given curve and the locus of its centre of spherical curvature respectively with

\overline{\textit{{r}}}=\textit{{r}}+R\textit{{n}}+(R^{\prime}T)\textit{{b}}~.

Now differentiating with respect to
s
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{\textit{{t}}}\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}+R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+R\left(-\frac{1}{R}\textit{{t}}+\frac{1}{T}\textit{{b}}\right)+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+(R^{\prime}T)\left(-\frac{1}{T}\textit{{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{R}{T}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\right]\textit{{b}}~,

where
\overline{s}
is the arc length of the locus of the centre of spherical curvature. So we can write

\overline{\textit{{t}}}=\lambda\textit{{b}}~.

Again differentiating with respect to
s
, we get

(\overline{\kappa}\overline{\textit{{n}}})\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}=-\lambda\tau\textit{{n}}~,

i.e.
,
\overline{\textit{{n}}}
is parallel to
n
.

Solution 3.17: A curve is a spherical curve if and only if its osculating sphere is a constant sphere. For the osculating sphere
S
, the centre
w
and radius
\sigma
are given by

\textit{{w}}=\textit{{r}}+R\textit{{n}}+(R^{\prime}T)\textit{{b}}~~~\mbox{and}~~~\sigma^{2}=R^{2}+(R^{\prime}T)^{2}~.

The osculating sphere is a constant sphere if its centre is fixed and radius is constant,
i.e.
,

\textit{{w}}^{\prime}=0~~~\mbox{and}~~~\sigma^{\prime}=0~.

Now,

\displaystyle\textit{{w}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\textit{{w}}}{ds}=\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}+R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+R\frac{d\textit{{n}}}{ds}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+(R^{\prime}T)\frac{d\textit{{b}}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}+R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+R(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+(R^{\prime}T)\left(-\frac{1}{T}\textit{{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}+R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}-\textit{{t}}+\frac{R}{T}\textit{{b}}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\textit{{b}}-R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{R}{T}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\right]\textit{{b}}

and

(\sigma^{2})^{\prime}=2\sigma\sigma^{\prime}=2RR^{\prime}+2(R^{\prime}T)(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}=2(R^{\prime}T)\left[\frac{R}{T}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}\right]~.

Thus we see that the centre is fixed and the radius is constant if and only if

\frac{R}{T}+(R^{\prime}T)^{\prime}=0~.

This is the necessary and sufficient condition for the curve to be a spherical curve.

Solution 3.18: We have

\overline{\textit{{r}}}=\textit{{r}}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\textit{{n}}~~~(\kappa=\mbox{constant}).

Differentiating with respect to
s
, the arc length of the twisted curve
M
, we get

\overline{\textit{{t}}}\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}=\overline{t}+\frac{1}{\kappa}(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})=\frac{\tau}{\kappa}\textit{{b}}~.

As both
t
and
b
are unit vectors, so choosing the direction of increment of
\overline{s}
properly, we have

\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}=\frac{\tau}{\kappa}~~~\mbox{and}~~~\overline{\textit{{t}}}=\textit{{b}}~.

Now differentiating the above second relation with respect to
s
, we have

\overline{\kappa}\overline{\textit{{n}}}\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}=-\tau\textit{{n}}~,~~i.e.,~~\overline{\kappa}\overline{\textit{{n}}}\left(\frac{\tau}{\kappa}\right)=-\tau\textit{{n}}~,~~i.e.,\overline{\kappa}\overline{\textit{{n}}}=-\kappa\textit{{n}}~~(\mbox{Since}~\tau\neq 0).

As both
\kappa
and
\overline{\kappa}
are positive, so we have

\overline{\kappa}=\kappa~~~\mbox{and}~~~\overline{\textit{{n}}}=-\textit{{n}}~.

Again,

\overline{\textit{{b}}}=\overline{\textit{{t}}}\times\overline{\textit{{n}}}=\textit{{b}}\times(-\textit{{n}})=\textit{{t}}~.

Differentiating this relation, we have

\displaystyle-\overline{\tau}\overline{\textit{{n}}}\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle i.e.,~-\overline{\tau}\overline{\textit{{n}}}\frac{\tau}{\kappa}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~~~~~\kappa^{2}~

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\overline{\tau}

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~~~~~\overline{\kappa}~~

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa=\sqrt{\tau\overline{\tau}}~.

Also the position vector
\overline{\overline{\textit{{r}}}}
of the centre of circular curvature of the second curve is

\overline{\overline{\textit{{r}}}}=\overline{\textit{{r}}}+\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}}\overline{\textit{{n}}}=\left(\textit{{r}}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\textit{{n}}\right)-\frac{1}{\kappa}\textit{{n}}=\textit{{r}}~.

Hence the result.

Solution 3.19: Let the principal normal of a curve
C(\textit{{r}})
be the binormal of another curve
\overline{C}(\overline{\textit{{r}}})
. So we write

\overline{\textit{{r}}}=\textbf{{r}}+\lambda\textit{{n}}~,

(3.63)

where
\lambda
is some scalar function of the arc length
s
and
n
at
r
and
\overline{\textit{{b}}}
at
\overline{\textit{{r}}}
are collinear. Now differentiating eq. (
3.63
) with respect to the arc length
s
of
C
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{\textit{{t}}}\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{{t}}+\lambda^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\lambda(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})

(3.64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\kappa\lambda)\textit{{t}}+\lambda^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\lambda\tau\textit{{b}}~.

As
\overline{\textit{{t}}}
is orthogonal to
\overline{\textit{{b}}}
, which is parallel to
n
, so
\overline{\textit{{t}}}
is perpendicular to
n
. So from the above eq. (
3.64
), we have

\lambda^{\prime}=0~,~~i.e.,~~\lambda=~\mbox{a constant.}

Again differentiating (
3.64
) with respect to
s
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\textit{{t}}}\frac{d^{2}\overline{s}}{ds^{2}}+\overline{\kappa}\overline{\textit{{n}}}\left(\frac{d\overline{s}}{ds}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa^{\prime}\lambda\textit{{t}}+(1-\kappa\lambda)\kappa\textit{{n}}+\lambda\tau^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+\lambda\tau(-\tau\textit{{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\kappa^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+(\kappa-\kappa^{2}\lambda-\lambda\tau^{2})\textit{{n}}+\lambda\tau^{\prime}\textit{{b}}~.

Note that the L.H.S. is orthogonal to
\overline{\textit{{b}}}
,
i.e.
, to
n
, hence the component of
n
should be zero in the R.H.S.. So we have

\displaystyle\kappa-\kappa^{2}\lambda-\lambda\tau^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~~~~\lambda(\kappa^{2}+\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa.

Hence the result.

Solution 3.20: The equation of the osculating plane at the point
P(\textit{{r}})
of a curve may be written as

\left[\textit{{R}}-\textit{{r}},\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}}\right]=0~,

where
R
is the current point on the osculating plane. Let
A(\textit{{a}})
be the common point of all the osculating planes. Then we have

\left[\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}},\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}}\right]=0~.

(3.65)

Now differentiating the above equation with respect to ‘
s
’ we get

\left[-\textit{{t}},\textit{{t}},\textit{{n}}\right]+\left[\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}},\kappa\textit{{n}},\textit{{n}}\right]+\left[\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}},\textit{{t}},-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}\right]=0

i.e.,~~~~~~\tau\left[\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}},\textit{{t}},\textit{{b}}\right]=0~.

If
\tau=0
then the curve is a plane curve, otherwise we have

\left[\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}},\textit{{t}},\textit{{b}}\right]=0~.

(3.66)

The relations (3.56) and (3.57) suggest that:

\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}}
is orthogonal to
\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{n}}~~i.e.,~~\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}}\perp\textit{{b}}
and
\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}}
is orthogonal to
\textit{{t}}\times\textit{{b}}

i.e.
,
\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}}\perp\textit{{n}}
.

Hence
\textit{{a}}-\textit{{r}}
is parallel to
t

i.e.
,

\frac{dx^{1}}{x^{1}-a^{1}}=\frac{dx^{2}}{x^{2}-a^{2}}=\frac{dx^{3}}{x^{3}-a^{3}}=\lambda^{\prime}~~(\mbox{say})

(3.67)

where
\textit{{a}}=(a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})
is a given vector.

Now integrating equation (
3.67
) we get

\log|x^{i}-a^{i}|=\lambda(s)+\log c^{i}

\mbox{or,}~~~~\frac{x^{i}-a^{i}}{c^{i}}=e^{\lambda(s)}

or equivalently,

\frac{x^{1}-a^{1}}{c^{1}}=\frac{x^{2}-a^{2}}{c^{2}}=\frac{x^{3}-a^{3}}{c^{3}}

i.e.
, the curve is a plane curve.

Solution 3.21: We have

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{n}\textit{{t}}+b_{n}\textit{{n}}+c_{n}\textit{{b}}

\displaystyle\textit{{r}}^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{n}^{\prime}\textit{{t}}+a_{n}(\kappa\textit{{n}})+b_{n}^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+b_{n}(-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}})+c_{n}^{\prime}\textit{{b}}+c_{n}(-\tau\textit{{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a_{n}^{\prime}-\kappa b_{n})\textit{{t}}+(\kappa a_{n}+b_{n}^{\prime}-\tau c_{n})\textit{{n}}+(\tau b_{n}+c_{n}^{\prime})\textit{{b}}~.

But

\textit{{r}}^{\bm{n+1}}=a_{n+1}\textit{{t}}+b_{n+1}\textit{{n}}+c_{n+1}\textit{{b}}~.

Hence comparing the co-efficients we have

a_{n+1}=a_{n}^{\prime}-\kappa b_{n}~~,~~b_{n+1}=b_{n}^{\prime}+\kappa a_{n}-\tau c_{n}~~,~~c_{n+1}=c_{n}^{\prime}+\tau b_{n}~.

Solution 3.22: Let
a
be the unit vector along the given fixed direction. Then
\textit{{t}}\cdot\textit{{a}}=\cos\theta
and
\textit{{b}}\cdot\textit{{a}}=\cos\phi
. Thus we have

\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{a}}=-\sin\theta\,\frac{d\theta}{ds}~~\mbox{and}~~\textit{{b}}^{\prime}\cdot\textit{{a}}=-\sin\phi\,\frac{d\phi}{ds}

\mbox{or}~~~~\kappa(\textit{{n}}\cdot\textit{{a}})=-\sin\theta\,\frac{d\theta}{ds}~~\mbox{and}~~-\tau(\textit{{n}}\cdot\textit{{a}})=-\sin\phi\,\frac{d\phi}{ds}

\therefore~~~~\frac{\kappa}{\tau}=-\frac{\sin\theta\,d\theta}{\sin\phi\,d\phi}~.~~(\mbox{proved})

Solution 3.23: Let the consecutive points on the curve be
r
and
\textit{{r}}+d\textit{{r}}
and the principal normals be
n
and
\textit{{n}}+d\textit{{n}}
respectively. For intersection of the principal normals a necessary condition is that the 3 vectors
d\textit{{r}}~,~\textit{{n}}
and
\textit{{n}}+d\textit{{n}}
must be coplanar,
i.e.
,

\frac{d\textit{{r}}}{ds}~,~\textit{{n}}~,~\frac{d\textit{{n}}}{ds}~~\textrm{must be coplaner}.

i.e.,~~~~\left[\textit{{t}},~\textit{{n}},-\kappa\textit{{t}}+\tau\textit{{b}}\right]=0~~~\Rightarrow~~\tau=0

Solution 3.24:

\displaystyle LHS

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{T\left(R\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\right\}^{\prime}+\left(\frac{T}{R}\textit{{t}}\right)^{\prime}+\frac{R}{T}\left(\textit{{t}}^{\prime}\right)

(3.68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{T(R.\frac{1}{R}\textit{{n}})^{\prime}\right\}^{\prime}+\left(\frac{T}{R}\textit{{t}}\right)^{\prime}+\frac{R}{T}\cdot\frac{1}{R}\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{T\left(-\frac{1}{R}\textit{{t}}+\frac{1}{T}\textit{{b}}\right)\right\}^{\prime}+\left(\frac{T}{R}\textit{{t}}\right)^{\prime}+\frac{1}{T}\textit{{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{T}{R}\textit{{t}}\right)^{\prime}+\textit{{b}}^{\prime}+\left(\frac{T}{R}\textit{{t}}\right)^{\prime}-\textit{{b}}^{\prime}~~=~0~~=RHS.

Solution 3.25:

\bar{\textit{{r}}}=\textit{{r}}+R\textit{{n}}

So differentiating with respect to ‘
s
’ we have

\bar{\textit{{t}}}\frac{d\bar{s}}{ds}=\textit{{t}}+R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+R\left(-\frac{1}{R}\textit{{t}}+\frac{1}{T}\textit{{b}}\right)=R^{\prime}\textit{{n}}+\frac{R}{T}\textit{{b}}

Squaring both sides we have

\left(\frac{d\bar{s}}{ds}\right)^{2}=R^{\prime 2}+\left(\frac{R}{T}\right)^{2}~,~~\mbox{(proved).}

Chapter 4

Hypersurface in a Riemannian space

4.1

Basic Definition

A ‘
n
’ dimensional hypersurface
V_{n}
in an
(m+1)
-dimensional Riemannian space
M
is given by the equations

y^{\alpha}=f^{\alpha}(x^{1},x^{2},\ldots,x^{n}),~~\alpha=1,2,\ldots m+1

where
\{y^{\alpha}\}
is a co-ordinate system in
M
and
x^{i}
’s are
n
real variables such that the Jacobian matrix

J=\left[\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{\partial y^{1}}{\partial x^{1}}&\frac{\partial y^{1}}{\partial x^{2}}&\ldots&\frac{\partial y^{1}}{\partial x^{n}}\\
\frac{\partial y^{2}}{\partial x^{1}}&\frac{\partial y^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}&\ldots&\frac{\partial y^{2}}{\partial x^{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial y^{m+1}}{\partial x^{1}}&\frac{\partial y^{m+1}}{\partial x^{2}}&\ldots&\frac{\partial y^{m+1}}{\partial x^{n}}\end{array}\right]

is of rank
n
.

In particular if
m>n
then
V_{n}
is called a subspace of
M
or
M
is called an enveloping space of
V_{n}
. For
m=n
,
V_{n}
is called hypersurface of the enveloping space
M
.

Note: The
n
real variables
\{x^{i}\}
is a co-ordinate system in
V_{n}
.

Induced metric in
V_{n}
:

Let
a_{\alpha\beta}
be the components of the metric tensor in
M
under some
y-
co-ordinate system. Then the elementary distance ‘
dS
’ between two neighbouring points in
V_{n}
(which are therefore also in
M
) is given by

\displaystyle dS^{2}=a_{\alpha\beta}dy^{\alpha}dy^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}dx^{i}\right)\left(\frac{\partial y^{\beta}}{\partial x^{k}}dx^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(a_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial y^{\beta}}{\partial x^{k}}\right)dx^{i}dx^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{ik}dx^{i}dx^{k}~,

where

g_{ik}=a_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial y^{\beta}}{\partial x^{k}}

(4.1)

is the metric tensor in
V_{n}
.

Note: Similar to
a_{\alpha\beta}
,
g_{ik}
is also symmetric in ‘
i
’ and ‘
k
’.

Normal to the hypersurface:

Let
N^{\alpha}
be the contravariant components (in the
y-
co-ordinate system) of the unit normal
N
to
V_{n}\subset M
. For fixed
i~(i=1,2,\ldots,n)
the vector
\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}
is tangential to
V_{n}
and hence orthogonal to the normal vector
N
.

For another co-ordinate system
\bar{y}^{\alpha}
in
M
we write

\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}=\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial\bar{y}^{\beta}}\frac{\partial\bar{y}^{\beta}}{\partial x^{i}}~,

which shows that the tangential vector is a contravariant vector in
M
. Thus the orthogonality of normal vector
N
and the above tangent vector gives

a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}N^{\beta}=0

(4.2)

and the normalization of
N
gives

a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}=1~.

(4.3)

with
\bigtriangledown_{{}_{i}}y^{\alpha}=\dfrac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}
.

4.2

Generalized Intrinsic and Covariant Differentiation : Differentiation on the hypersurface

Let us start with the symbol convention : any Greek index stands for tensor character in
M
while any Latin index denotes tensor character in the hypersurface
V_{n}
. As we have seen
\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}=\dfrac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}
, for fixed
i
represents a contravariant vector in
y-
co-ordinates in
M
, so in a similar way, for fixed
\alpha
,
\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}
is a covariant vector in the
x-
coordinates in
V_{n}
.

Suppose ‘
s
’ be the arc length along any curve
\gamma
in
V_{n}
and
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}
be an arbitrary tensor field along
\gamma
. According to the above symbol convention the tensor field
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}
is a (1, 1)-tensor in
y-
coordinates in
M
and it is a (0, 1)-tensor in the
x-
coordinates in
V_{n}
. Let
u_{\alpha}
,
v^{\beta}
be the components in the
y-
coordinates of two unit vector fields parallel to
\gamma
with respect to
M
and
\omega^{i}
be the components in the
x-
coordinates of a unit vector field parallel to
\gamma
with respect to
V_{n}
. So we have

\displaystyle\frac{\delta u_{\alpha}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~~i.e.,~~\frac{du_{\alpha}}{ds}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\alpha\sigma}u_{\theta}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}=0

\displaystyle\frac{\delta v^{\beta}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~~i.e.,~~\frac{dv^{\beta}}{ds}+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\theta\sigma}v^{\theta}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}=0

\displaystyle\textrm{and}~~~~\frac{\delta\omega^{i}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~~i.e.,~~\frac{d\omega^{i}}{ds}+\Gamma^{i}_{pm}\omega^{p}\frac{dx^{m}}{ds}=0~.

Now we consider the intrinsic derivative of the scalar
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega_{i}

i.e.
, we start with

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{ds}(A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}(A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega^{i})=\frac{dA^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}\frac{du_{\alpha}}{ds}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}\frac{dv^{\beta}}{ds}\omega^{i}+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\frac{d\omega^{i}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dA^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}\left(\frac{du_{\alpha}}{ds}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\alpha\sigma}u_{\theta}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}\right)+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}\omega^{i}\left(\frac{dv^{\beta}}{ds}+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\theta\sigma}v^{\theta}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}\right)

\displaystyle+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\left(\frac{d\omega^{i}}{ds}+\Gamma^{i}_{pm}\omega^{p}\frac{dx^{m}}{ds}\right)+\Gamma^{\theta}_{\alpha\sigma}A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}u_{\theta}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{\beta}_{\theta\sigma}A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}\omega^{i}v^{\theta}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}-\Gamma^{i}_{pm}A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega^{p}\frac{dx^{m}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{dA^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\theta\sigma}A^{\theta}_{\beta i}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}A^{\alpha}_{\theta i}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}-\Gamma^{p}_{im}A^{\alpha}_{\beta p}\frac{dx^{m}}{ds}\right\}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}

\displaystyle+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}v^{\beta}\omega^{i}\frac{\delta u_{\alpha}}{ds}+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}\omega^{i}\frac{\delta v^{\beta}}{ds}+A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}u_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\frac{\delta\omega^{i}}{ds}~.

Using Leibnitz’s rule to the left hand side we have for arbitrary
u_{\alpha}~,~v^{\beta}
and
\omega^{i}

\frac{\delta A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}=\frac{dA^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\theta\sigma}A^{\theta}_{\beta i}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}A^{\alpha}_{\theta i}\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}-\Gamma^{p}_{im}A^{\alpha}_{\beta p}\frac{dx^{m}}{ds}

It is called the generalized intrinsic derivative of
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}
w.r.t ‘
s
’ (
i.e.
, along the curve
\gamma
). From the quotient law
\dfrac{\delta A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}
is of the same type as
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}
. If the functions
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}
are defined throughout
V_{n}
and
\gamma
is an arbitrary curve in
V_{n}
then we may write the R.H.S. of the above relation as

\frac{\delta A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{ds}=\left[\frac{\partial A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{\partial x^{m}}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\theta\sigma}A^{\theta}_{\beta i}\nabla_{m}y^{\sigma}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}A^{\alpha}_{\theta i}\nabla_{m}y^{\sigma}-\Gamma^{p}_{im}A^{\alpha}_{\beta p}\right]\frac{dx^{m}}{ds}

where we write
\nabla_{m}y^{\sigma}=\dfrac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{m}}
for convenience. As
\dfrac{dx^{m}}{ds}
is a contravariant vector in the x-coordinate system, so it follows that the expression within square bracket is a tensor of the type
A^{\alpha}_{\beta im}

i.e.
, a tensor of type (1, 1) in the y co-ordinate and of the kind (0, 2) in the
x
’s. We call it the generalized covariant
x^{m}
-derivative of
A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}
and we write

\nabla_{m}A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}=\frac{\partial A^{\alpha}_{\beta i}}{\partial x^{m}}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\theta\sigma}A^{\theta}_{\beta i}\nabla_{m}y^{\sigma}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}A^{\alpha}_{\theta i}\nabla_{m}y^{\sigma}-\Gamma^{p}_{im}A^{\alpha}_{\beta p}

Now as covariant derivative of metric tensor is zero so we have

\nabla_{m}g_{ij}=0~;~\nabla_{\sigma}a_{\alpha\beta}=0

We shall now show that
\nabla_{m}a_{\alpha\beta}=0
.

By definition

\displaystyle\nabla_{m}a_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{m}}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\alpha\sigma}a_{\theta\beta}\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{m}}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}a_{\alpha\theta}\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial a_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial y^{\sigma}}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\alpha\sigma}a_{\theta\beta}-\Gamma^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}a_{\alpha\theta}\right)\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{\sigma}a_{\alpha\beta}\right)\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{m}}=0.

Note: In general, we write

\nabla_{m}A^{\cdots\cdots}_{\cdots\cdots}=(\nabla_{\sigma}A^{\cdots\cdots}_{\cdots\cdots})\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{m}}~.

4.3

Gauss’s formula : Second Fundamental form

We denote

\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}=\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}

\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{h}_{ij}\nabla_{h}y^{\alpha}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\theta\sigma}\nabla_{i}y^{\theta}\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{j}}=\frac{\partial^{2}y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}-\Gamma^{h}_{ij}\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{h}}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\theta\sigma}\frac{\partial y^{\theta}}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{j}}

(4.4)

Note that equation (
4.4
) is symmetric in
(i,j)
.

As
g_{ij}=a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)
so taking generalized covariant derivative w.r.t.
x^{k}
we have

a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)+a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)=0~~~\left(\nabla_{k}g_{ij}=0~~\mbox{and}~~\nabla_{k}a_{\alpha\beta}=0\right).

Now rotating
(i,j,k)
cyclically we get two more similar equations:

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta})+a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}y^{\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\textrm{and}~~a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{i}y^{\beta})+a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}y^{\beta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Now subtracting the first of these three equations from the sum of the last two and dividing by 2 and remembering that
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}
is symmetric in i and j we get

a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta})=0.

(4.5)

Thus for any fixed i, j we see that
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}
is a vector in
V_{n+1}
and normal to
V_{n}
. So we write

\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}=b_{ij}N^{\alpha}~.

(4.6)

Equations (
4.4
) , (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) together are called Gauss’s formula. Here
b_{ij}
is a symmetric covariant hypersurface tensor of second order and
N^{\alpha}
is a unit normal to the hypersurface
V_{n}
. Also transvecting equation (
4.6
) by
a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\beta}
we get

b_{ij}(a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}N^{\beta})=a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha})N^{\beta}

i.e.,~~~~b_{ij}=a_{\alpha\beta}(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha})N^{\beta}

(4.7)

The elementary quadratic form
b_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}
is called the second fundamental form and
b_{ij}
, the second fundamental tensor or the shape tensor.

Note: If we choose
M=V_{n+1}=E_{n+1}
, the
(n+1)
-dimensional Euclidean space then
y
can be chosen to be rectangular Cartesian co-ordinates and equation (
4.7
) simplifies to

b_{ij}=a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)N^{\beta}=(\partial_{i}\textit{{e}}_{\bm{j}})\cdot\textit{{N}}~.

4.4

Meusnier’s Theorem and consequences

Theorem 4.1 : Meusnier’s Theorem :

Statement: If for a curve
\gamma
on a hypersurface
V_{n}
of
M
(
=V_{n+1}
) that passes through a point
P
and have a given direction there at, the first normal relative to
V_{n+1}
makes an angle
\theta
with the normal to the hypersurface then the expression
\kappa_{1(a)}\cos\theta
is an invariant for all such curves where
\kappa_{1(a)}
is the first curvature relatively to
V_{n+1}
.

Proof: Suppose the curve
\gamma
in
V_{n}
has a given direction at
P
. Let
t
be the unit tangent to the curve at
P
and let
t^{\alpha}
and
t^{i}
be the contravariant components of
t
in the
y
co-ordinate system in
V_{n+1}
and in the
x
-coordinate system in
V_{n}
respectively.

Then

t^{\alpha}=(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})t^{i}

Taking covariant derivative with respect to
x^{j}
we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}t^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})t^{i}+(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{j}t^{i})

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~(\nabla_{\sigma}t^{\alpha})\frac{\partial y^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b_{ij}N^{\alpha})t^{i}+(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{j}t^{i})~.

Now transvecting both side with
\dfrac{dx^{j}}{ds}
(
s
is the arc length of the curve) and noting that
t^{i}=\dfrac{dx^{i}}{ds}
, we get

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\sigma}t^{\alpha})\frac{dy^{\sigma}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds})N^{\alpha}+(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})(\nabla_{j}t^{i})\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~~~~~\frac{\delta t^{\alpha}}{ds}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds})N^{\alpha}+(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})\frac{\delta t^{i}}{ds}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~\kappa_{1(a)}n_{1(a)}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}N^{\alpha}+\kappa_{1(g)}n_{1(g)}^{i}(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha})

(4.9)

where
\kappa_{1(a)}\longrightarrow
the first curvature scalar of the curve relative to
V_{n+1}

\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(a)}}\longrightarrow
the first normal vector to
\gamma
relative to
V_{n+1}
.

\kappa_{1(g)}\longrightarrow
the first curvature scalar of
\gamma
relative to
V_{n}

\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(g)}}\longrightarrow
the first normal vector to
\gamma
relative to
V_{n}

\mbox{and}~~~~\kappa_{n}=b_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}~,

(4.10)

is the normal curvature.

In vector notation, equation (
4.9
) can be written as

\kappa_{1(a)}\textit{{n}}_{1(a)}=\kappa_{n}\textit{{N}}+\kappa_{1(g)}\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(g)}}

(4.11)

Now taking scalar product with
N
(the unit normal to the hypersurface
V_{n}
) we get

\kappa_{1(a)}\cos\theta=\kappa_{n}~.

(4.12)

From the expression (
4.10
) we note that
\kappa_{n}
is independent of the curve
\gamma
, it depends only on the direction of the tangent at the point
P
. Hence
\kappa_{1(a)}\cos\theta
is an invariant for all curves in
V_{n}
, passing through
P
and having the given direction there at. Hence the theorem.

Theorem 4.2 : Darboux’s Theorem :

Statement: For a curve
\gamma
in a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
that passes through a given point
P
and have a given direction at
P
, the projection of the first curvature vector relatively to
V_{n+1}
upon the tangent space of
V_{n}
at the point concerned is equal to the first curvature vector relatively to
V_{n}
.

Proof: In Meusnier’s theorem, equation (
4.11
) can be interpreted as follows:

The first curvature vector
\kappa_{1(a)}\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(a)}}
relatively to
V_{n+1}
can be resolved into two orthogonal components:

i) the component
\kappa_{n}\textit{{N}}
along the normal to the hypersurface at
P
.

ii) the components
\kappa_{1(g)}\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(g)}}
in the tangent space
T_{P}
at
P
to the hypersurface. Hence the theorem.

Note:
\kappa_{n}
is called the normal curvature of the curve at
P
in the particular direction.
\kappa_{1(g)}\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(g)}}
is called the first curvature vector of the curve relatively to
V_{n}
. Also it is called the geodesic first curvature vector or Darboux vector. Its magnitude
\kappa_{1(g)}
is the first curvature of the curve relatively to
V_{n}
and is also called geodesic first curvature of the curve.

Theorem 4.3 : Another result from Meusnier’s theorem :

Statement: A curve
\gamma
on a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
is a geodesic in
V_{n}
iff at every point of
\gamma
the first curvature vector relatively to
V_{n+1}
is normal to
V_{n}
. Further, for a geodesic, its first curvature relatively to
V_{n+1}
is equal to the normal curvature of the hypersurface in the direction of the geodesic.

Proof: From equation (
4.11
) we note that if for a curve through
P
having the given direction there at, the first curvature vector relatively to
V_{n+1}
has the direction of
N
then

\kappa_{1(a)}\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(a)}}=\kappa_{n}\textit{{N}}

and hence for such curve
\kappa_{1(a)}=\kappa_{n}
. Hence the first part.

For the second part, we have again from equation (
4.11
) , considering the magnitude,

\kappa_{1(a)}^{2}=\kappa_{n}^{2}+\kappa_{1(g)}^{2}

Thus at any point
\kappa_{1(a)}=\kappa_{n}
implies
\kappa_{1(g)}=0
.

If this happen at every point of the curve, then

\frac{\delta t^{i}}{ds}=0~~\textrm{(identically)}

and the curve is a geodesic in
V_{n}
. Also in this case

\kappa_{1(a)}\textit{{n}}_{\bm{1(a)}}=\kappa_{n}\textit{{N}}.

Hence the theorem.

Note: We often denote
\kappa_{n}
by
\chi
.

4.5

Principal curvatures and Principal directions

At a point
P
on the hypersurface
V_{n}
, a direction
t
in which the normal curvature
\chi
attains an extreme value (local extreme) is called a principal direction and the extreme value of the normal curvature is called a principal curvature.

By definition,

\chi=b_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}~.

As
t
is a unit vector so
g_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}=1.

Hence we have

\chi=\frac{b_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}}{g_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}}~.

(4.13)

Now, for variation of the direction
t
, the extreme values (local extrema) of
\chi
are given by

\displaystyle\frac{d\chi}{dt^{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~,~~~i=1,2,\ldots,n

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~\left(g_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}\right)\left(b_{ij}t^{j}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(b_{ij}t^{i}t^{j}\right)\left(g_{ij}t^{j}\right)=0

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~\left(b_{ij}-\chi g_{ij}\right)t^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~.

(4.14)

Thus every direction at
P
will be a principal direction if

b_{ij}-\chi g_{ij}=0

(4.15)

In this case
b_{ij}
are proportional to
g_{ij}
and
\chi
is independent of the direction
t
at such a point. This point is called an
umbilic
.

Suppose that the point
P
is not an umbilic. Then solutions will be obtained for values of
\chi
given by the equation

\left|b_{ij}-\chi g_{ij}\right|=0

(4.16)

This is called the characteristic equation for
b_{ij}
in the metric of the hypersurface. Since
b_{ij}
and
g_{ij}
are both real symmetric and
g_{ij}
is positive definite so the above characteristic will have n real roots
\chi_{h}~(h=1,2,\ldots n)
with or without repetitions. These are called the
n
principal curvatures. Any value of
t^{i}
corresponding to any root is a principal direction. The principal directions corresponding to unequal roots of
\chi
will be orthogonal to each other. On the other hand, if
\chi_{P}
is a repeated root of multiplicity
‘
r
’ then the solution space of
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{P}}
is of dimension ‘
r
’ and we can choose in multiply infinite number of ways ‘
r
’ mutually orthogonal directions for
t
and these principal directions will also be orthogonal to other principal directions corresponding to other principal curvatures. Thus there always exists ‘
n
’ mutually orthogonal principal directions at any point
P
in a hypersurface
V_{n}
.

Note: If every point of a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
is an umbilic then the hypersurface is said to be a totally umbilical hypersurface. This is the generalization of the notion of a sphere or a plane in
E_{3}
or a hypersphere or a hyperplane in
E_{n+1}
.

Theorem 4.4 : Prove that any two distinct principal directions in the neighbourhood U of a point P of a hypersurface
V_{n}
are mutually orthogonal.

Proof: From equation (
4.5
)

b_{ij}t^{j}=\chi g_{ij}t^{j}

Let
\chi_{1}~,\chi_{2}
be the principal normal curvatures and
t_{1}^{i}
and
t_{2}^{i}
be the corresponding two distinct principal directions. So we have

b_{ij}t_{1}^{j}=\chi_{1}g_{ij}t_{1}^{j}

(4.17)

and

b_{ij}t_{2}^{j}=\chi_{2}g_{ij}t_{2}^{j}

(4.18)

Now, (
4.17
)
\times~t_{2}^{i}

-
(
4.18
)
\times~t_{1}^{i}
gives

(\chi_{1}-\chi_{2})g_{ij}t_{1}^{i}t_{2}^{j}=0~,~~~\textrm{using symmetric property of}~b_{ij}~\mbox{and}~g_{ij}~.

As

\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}

so

g_{ij}t_{1}^{i}t_{2}^{j}=0

i.e.
,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{1}}
is orthogonal to
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{2}}
.

4.6

Mean curvatures of different orders and the total curvature

Let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\ldots\chi_{n}
be the ‘
n
’ principal curvatures at
P
of a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
. Then the sum of the products of the principal curvatures
\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\ldots\chi_{n}
taken ‘
p
’ (
\leq n
) at a time is called the
mean curvature
of order ‘
p
’ or the
p
-th mean curvature and it will be denoted by
M_{P}
. The first mean curvature
M_{1}=\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}+\ldots+\chi_{n}
is denoted by
M
and is called simply the mean curvature. The product
\chi_{1}\cdot\chi_{2}\cdots\chi_{n}
of all the principal curvatures
i.e.
, the
n
-th mean curvature
M_{n}
is also called the total curvature or the Gaussian curvature and is denoted by
\mathcal{K}
.

The characteristic equation,

\left|b_{ij}-\chi g_{ij}\right|=0

can be written as

\left|g^{ki}\right|\left|b_{ij}-\chi g_{ij}\right|=0

(as the metric tensor
g_{ij}
is non-singular so is also
g^{ij}
)

So we have

\left|b^{k}_{j}-\chi\delta^{k}_{j}\right|=0

The above determinant in explicit form is given by

\chi^{n}-b_{1}\chi^{n-1}+\cdots+(-1)^{p}b_{p}\chi^{n-p}+\cdots+(-1)^{n}b_{n}=0

where
b_{p}
is the sum of the principal minor of order ‘
p
’ of the matrix
b^{k}_{i}
and may be termed as the trace of order
p
or the
p
-th trace of the matrix. So from the definition we have

b_{p}=M_{p}~~,~\forall p=1,2,\ldots n~.

In particular,

M=M_{1}=b^{i}_{i}=g^{ij}b_{ij}

and

\mathcal{K}=M_{n}=\left|b^{k}_{i}\right|=\left|g^{ki}b_{ij}\right|=\left|g^{ki}\right|\left|b_{ij}\right|=\frac{b}{g}

If
M=0
then the surface is called a minimal surface.

4.7

Conjugate directions : Asymptotic line and Asymptotic direction

Two vectors
u
and
v
at any point on the hypersurface are said to be
conjugate
if
b_{ij}u^{i}v^{j}=0
. The directions, of the vectors
u
and
v
are said to be
conjugate directions
.

A self conjugate direction at any point is called an
asymptotic direction
at that point and a curve at any point of which the tangent direction is an asymptotic direction is called an
asymptotic line
. It is clear that in a hypersurface there can be a real asymptotic line iff the second fundamental form is not definite (
i.e.
, neither positive nor negative definite). The differential equation of an asymptotic line is

b_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}=0

(4.19)

It follows that a curve in the hypersurface is an asymptotic line if
\kappa_{n}=0
at every point of the curve in the direction of the curve there at.

Theorem 4.5 : If two principal directions are orthogonal at a point of the hypersurface then they are not only orthogonal but are also conjugate.

Proof: Let
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p}}
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{q}}
be two principal directions at a point on the hypersurface and are orthogonal to each other. Suppose
\chi_{p}
and
\chi_{q}
are the corresponding principal curvatures. Then from equation (4.14) we have

(b_{ij}-\chi_{p}g_{ij})t^{j}_{p}=0

Now, multiply this equation by
t^{i}_{q}
and summing over
i
we get

b_{ij}t^{j}_{p}t^{i}_{q}=\chi_{p}g_{ij}t_{q}^{i}t^{j}_{p}

By condition,
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p}}
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{q}}
are orthogonal so the R.H.S. vanishes. Hence
b_{ij}t_{p}^{j}t_{q}^{i}=0

i.e.,

\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p}}
and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{q}}
are conjugate to each other.

Theorem 4.6 : The normal curvature of the hypersurface
V_{n}
for an asymptotic direction is zero.

Proof: The normal curvature
\kappa_{n}
of the hypersurface
V_{n}
in the direction of a curve
\gamma
is given by

\kappa_{n}=b_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}

As
\gamma
is an asymptotic line of
V_{n}
(by condition) so we have
b_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}=0~~i.e.,~~\kappa_{n}=0
.

Theorem 4.7 : Euler’s Theorem :

Statement: If
\chi_{{}_{1}},\chi_{{}_{2}},\ldots,\chi_{{}_{n}}
are
n
principal curvatures distinct or otherwise and
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{1}}}~,\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{2}}}~,\ldots,\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{n}}}
are mutually orthogonal principal directions corresponding to these principal curvatures, then the normal curvature
\chi
in the direction
l
making an angle
\theta_{i}
with the direction
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{i}}}~~,~~(i=1,2,\ldots n)
is given by

\chi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{i}\cos^{2}\theta_{i}

Proof: We have
\chi=b_{ij}l^{i}l^{j}

As
l
can be written as a linear combination of the principal directions so

\textit{{l}}=\lambda_{1}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{1}}}+\lambda_{2}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{2}}}+\ldots+\lambda_{n}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{n}}}

Hence

\displaystyle\textit{{l}}\cdot\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{k}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}~~i.e.,~~\lambda_{k}=\cos\theta_{k}

l

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{k}}}\cos\theta_{k}

\displaystyle\mbox{and}~~~~~~\chi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{ij}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{{p_{k}}}}^{i}\cos\theta_{k}\right)\left(\sum_{r=1}^{n}\textit{{t}}_{\bm{{p_{r}}}}^{j}\cos\theta_{r}\right)

As two orthogonal principal directions are also conjugate to each other so we have

b_{ij}t_{p_{k}}^{i}t_{p_{r}}^{j}=0~~(k\neq r)~~~\mbox{and}~~~b_{ij}t_{p_{k}}^{i}t_{p_{k}}^{j}=\chi_{k}~.

Thus the expression for
\chi
gives

\chi=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\chi_{k}\cos^{2}\theta_{k}

Note :
As the sum of principal curvatures is the sum of normal curvatures for
n
mutually orthogonal directions in
V_{n}
so the above sum may be the sum of the normal curvatures in any
n
mutually orthogonal directions (Dupin’s Theorem below)

Theorem 4.8 : Dupin’s Theorem :

Statement: At any point of a hyper surface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
, the sum of the normal curvatures in n mutually orthogonal directions is a constant, the mean curvature at that point.

Proof: Let
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{1}}},\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{2}}},\ldots,\textit{{t}}_{\bm{p_{n}}}
be a set of
n
orthogonal directions in
V_{n}
. Then sum of the normal curvatures of
V_{n}
for these orthogonal directions is

\sum_{p_{k}=1}^{n}b_{ij}t_{p_{k}}^{i}t_{p_{k}}^{j}=b_{ij}g^{ij}=M,~~\mbox{a constant.}

Hence the theorem.

Theorem 4.9 : A curve on a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
is a geodesic in
V_{n+1}
,
iff
it is a geodesic as well as an asymptotic line in
V_{n}
.

Proof: From Meusnier’s theorem

\kappa_{1(a)}^{2}=\kappa_{n}^{2}+\kappa_{1(g)}^{2}

Now,
\kappa_{1(a)}=0
implies
\kappa_{n}=0
and
\kappa_{1(g)}=0
and vice-versa. Hence it follows that the curve is a geodesic in
V_{n+1}
iff it is a geodesic as well as an asymptotic line in
V_{n}
.

Totally geodesic hypersurface

If all the geodesic of a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
are also geodesic of
V_{n+1}
then the hypersurface is called a
totally geodesic hypersurface
of the enveloping plane.

Theorem 4.10 : A hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
is totally geodesic in
V_{n+1}

iff

b_{ij}=0
identically.

Proof: From Meusnier’s theorem we have

\kappa_{1(a)}^{2}=\kappa_{n}^{2}+\kappa_{1(g)}^{2}

Now whenever
\kappa_{1(g)}=0
for any curve in
V_{n}
in any direction at
P
we must have
\kappa_{1(a)}=0
at
P
in that direction
iff

\kappa_{n}=0
at
P
in every direction there at.

By definition,

\kappa_{n}=b_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}

So
\kappa_{n}\equiv 0
implies
b_{ij}\equiv 0
.

Note I : This notion is a generalization of the notion of a plane in
E_{3}
or hyperplane in
E_{n+1}
.

Note II : The above theorem can be generalized as follows :

Theorem 4.11 : A totally geodesic hypersurface is a minimal hypersurface and its lines of curvature are indeterminate.

Proof: We have seen that
b_{ij}=0
identically for a totally geodesic hypersurface. So
M=b_{ij}g^{ij}=0
,
i.e.,
the hypersurface
V_{n}
is a minimal hypersurface. Consequently, the equation

b_{ij}=\frac{M}{n}g^{ij}

is identically satisfied. But it is the condition for indeterminant lines of curvature. Hence the theorem.

4.8

Weingarten’s Formula

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

(4.20)

Proof: We have

a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}=1.

Taking covariant derivative with respect to
x^{i}
we have

a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}\,\nabla_{i}N^{\beta}=0~.

This shows that the vectors
\nabla_{1}N^{\beta},~\nabla_{2}N^{\beta},\ldots,\nabla_{n}N^{\beta}
are orthogonal to
N
and therefore are hypersurface vectors. Hence each of them is a linear combination of the vectors
\nabla_{1}y^{\alpha},~\nabla_{2}y^{\alpha},\ldots,~\nabla_{n}y^{\alpha}
. Thus we write

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=t_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}.

(4.21)

Transvecting with
a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}
we get

a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)=t_{i}^{k}g_{kj}~.

As
a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}=0
, so taking covariant derivative with respect to
x^{i}
we have

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)=0

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}b_{ij}N^{\beta}=-\left(a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}\right)b_{ij}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}t_{i}^{k}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{ij}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~~~~~~~~~t_{i}^{k}g_{kj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{ij}~~~,~i.e.,~~~b_{ij}=-t_{ij}

So from equation (
4.21
),
\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}
, the Weingarten’s formula.

Note: In Eucledian space, the Weingarten’s formula takes the form :
\partial_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b_{i}^{k}\partial_{k}y^{\alpha}
.

Theorem 4.12 : The derived vector of the unit normal with respect to
V_{n+1}
, along a curve
\gamma
in
V_{n}
, will be tangential to the curve
\gamma
provided
\gamma
is a line of curvature of the hypersurface.

Proof: Let
t^{i}
be a unit tangent vector to a curve
\gamma
in
V_{n}
. Then the derived vector of
N^{\alpha}
with respect to
V_{n+1}
along
\gamma
in
V_{n}
is
\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)t^{i}
. But from Weingarten’s formula

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

\mbox{or,}~~~~\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)t^{i}=-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}t^{i}

By condition of the theorem
\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)t^{i}
will be along
t^{i}
implies

\displaystyle-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}t^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-Ky_{,i}^{\alpha}t^{i}~~~~,~~k~\mbox{is any constant}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~b_{ij}g^{jk}y_{,k}^{\alpha}t^{i}\left(a_{\alpha\beta}y^{\beta}_{,h}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Ky_{,i}^{\alpha}t^{i}\left(a_{\alpha\beta}y^{\beta}_{,h}\right)

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~b_{ij}g^{jk}t^{i}g_{kh}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Kg_{ih}t^{i}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~b_{ih}t^{i}-Kg_{ih}t^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~\left(b_{ih}-Kg_{ih}\right)t^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

This implies that the directions
t^{i}
is a principal direction for the symmetric tensor
b_{ij}
,
i.e.,

t^{i}
is a principal direction for the hypersurface. But
t^{i}
is a unit tangent to the curve
\gamma
in
V_{n}
. Hence
\gamma
is a line of curvature in
V_{n}
.

Theorem 4.13 : Prove that the normal to a totally geodesic hypersurface is parallel in the enveloping manifold.

Proof: From Weingarten’s formula (
4.20
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)e^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{i}^{k}e^{i}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

(4.22)

where
e^{i}
is any unit vector in the hypersurface
V_{n}
.

By condition,
V_{n}
is a totally geodesic hypersurface,
i.e.
, each geodesic of
V_{n}
is a geodesic in
V_{n+1}
.

Hence,
\kappa_{(a)}=0=\kappa_{(g)}
. But
\kappa_{(a)}^{2}=\kappa_{(n)}^{2}+\kappa_{(g)}^{2}
. So
\kappa_{(n)}=0
,
i.e.
,

b_{ij}\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}\frac{dx^{j}}{ds}=0.

But
\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}
is arbitrary, so
b_{ij}=0
. Hence from (
4.8
),
\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}
is orthogonal to
e^{i}
. This proves the theorem.

Rodrigues’ Formula: Along a line of curvature in
V_{n}
embedded in
V_{n+1}

d\textit{{N}}+\chi d\textit{{y}}=0~~,

where
\chi
is the normal curvature of
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
in the direction of the line of curvature.

Proof: By Weingarten’s formula

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}~.

Taking inner product with
\frac{dx^{i}}{ds}=t^{i}
, we have

\frac{\delta N^{\alpha}}{ds}=-\left(b_{i}^{k}t^{i}\right)\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{k}}~.

(4.23)

Now, along a line of curvature we have,

\displaystyle\left(b_{ij}-\chi g_{ij}\right)t^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~\left(b_{j}^{k}-\chi\delta_{j}^{k}\right)t^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle i.e.,~~~b_{j}^{k}t^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi t^{k}

Then from equation (
4.23
)

\frac{\delta N^{\alpha}}{ds}=-\chi t^{k}\frac{\partial y^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{k}}=-\chi\frac{dy^{\alpha}}{ds}

i.e.,~~\delta\textit{{N}}+\chi d\textit{{y}}=0

Hence the theorem.

Note: The above theorem (
i.e.
, Rodrigues’ formula) can be interpreted geometrically as follows :

Along a line of curvature, the normal to the hypersurface bends in the direction of the curve and the amount of bending is equal to
\chi
-times the displacement along the curve in the opposite sense.

Theorem 4.14 : The derived vector of the unit normal in an asymptotic direction of a hypersurface in a Riemannian space is orthogonal to that direction.

Proof: Let
l
be an asymptotic direction at the current point
P
of a hypersurface in a Riemannian space. Then

b_{ij}l^{i}l^{j}=0.

Now the derived vector of the unit normal
N^{\alpha}
to the hypersurface in the direction
l
is
l^{i}\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}
.
The scalar product of this vector with the vector
l
is

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}\left(l^{i}\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)\left(l^{j}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)l^{i}l^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-a_{\alpha\beta}\left(b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)l^{i}l^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{i}^{k}\left\{a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)\right\}l^{i}l^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{i}^{k}g_{kj}l^{i}l^{j}=-b_{ij}l^{i}l^{j}=0

This shows that the derived vector
l^{i}\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}
of the unit normal
N^{\alpha}
in the direction of
l
is orthogonal to
l
. Hence the theorem.

Theorem 4.15 : Prove that the normal to a totally geodesic hypersurface is parallel in the enveloping manifold.

Proof: From Weingarten’s formula :

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b_{i}^{k}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

\mbox{or,}~~~\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)e^{i}=-b_{i}^{k}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}\right)\cdot e^{i}

where,
e^{i}
is any unit vector of the hypersurface
V_{n}
.
As
V_{n}
is a totally geodesic hypersurface, so each geodesic of
V_{n}
is a geodesic of
V_{n+1}
,
i.e.
,
\kappa_{(a)}=0=\kappa_{(g)}
. But we have
\kappa_{(a)}^{2}=\kappa_{n}^{2}+\kappa_{(g)}^{2}
. Hence
\kappa_{n}=0
,
i.e.
,
b_{ij}\dfrac{dx^{i}}{ds}\dfrac{dx^{j}}{ds}=0
.

As
\dfrac{dx^{i}}{ds}
is arbitrary, so
b_{ij}=0
,
i.e.
,
\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)\cdot e^{i}=0

i.e.
,
\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}
is orthogonal to
e^{i}

i.e.
,
\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}
is orthogonal to any direction
e^{i}
of a totally geodesic hypersurface
V_{n}
. Hence the result.

4.9

Lines of Curvature : Differential Form

A curve in a hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
at every point of which the direction of tangent is a principal direction, is called a line of curvature.

We shall now determine the differential equation of the lines of curvature for 2-dimensional hypersurface
V_{2}
in
E_{3}
.

Note: Through every point of
V_{n}
there are
n
mutually orthogonal lines of curvature.

From equation (
4.5
) we have

b_{ij}t^{j}=\chi g_{ij}t^{j}~.

Now, putting
i=1,~2
, we get

b_{1j}t^{j}=\chi g_{1j}t^{j}

b_{2i}t^{i}=\chi g_{2i}t^{i}~.

So eliminating
\chi
between these two equations we obtain

\left(g_{1j}b_{2i}-g_{2i}b_{1j}\right)t^{i}t^{j}=0

\left(g_{1i}b_{2j}-g_{2i}b_{1j}\right)t^{i}t^{j}=0~.

As
t^{i}=\frac{du^{i}}{ds}
, the differential equation for the lines of curvature becomes

\left(g_{1i}b_{2j}-g_{2i}b_{1j}\right)du^{i}du^{j}=0

or in explicit form :

\left(g_{11}b_{12}-g_{12}b_{11}\right)\left(du^{1}\right)^{2}+\left(g_{11}b_{22}-g_{22}b_{11}\right)du^{1}du^{2}+\left(g_{12}b_{22}-g_{22}b_{12}\right)\left(du^{2}\right)^{2}=0

(4.24)

This is the differential equation of the line of curvatures in two dimensional hypersurface
V_{2}
in
E_{3}
.

Minimal hypersurface: A hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
of vanishing mean curvature is called a minimal hypersurface.

Note: The reason for this name is the fact that given a closed curve in
E_{3}
, the surface of minimal area bounded by the curve is a surface of vanishing mean curvature.

An useful formula for
M
for a hypersurface
V_{2}
in
E_{3}

M=g^{ij}b_{ij}=g^{11}b_{11}+2g^{12}b_{12}+g^{22}b_{22},

where

g^{ij}=\mbox{cofactor of}~\frac{g_{ji}}{g}~~~,~~g=\left|g_{ij}\right|=\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
g_{21}&g_{22}\end{array}\right|.

So,

g^{11}=\frac{g_{22}}{g}~~,~~g^{12}=-\frac{g_{12}}{g}~~,~~g^{22}=\frac{g_{11}}{g}~.

Hence,

M=\frac{1}{g}\left(g_{11}b_{22}-2g_{12}b_{12}+g_{22}b_{11}\right)

(4.25)

Theorem 4.16 : Prove that the mean curvature of a hypersurface is equal to the negative of the divergence of the unit normal.

Proof: Suppose
N^{\alpha}
be the unit normal vector to thr hypersurface
V_{n}
in
V_{n+1}
. Let
t^{\alpha}_{h}~~(h=1,2,\ldots,n)
be the ‘
n
’ unit tangent vectors in
V_{n+1}
to
n
congruences
e_{h}~(h=1,2,\ldots,n)
of an orthogonal ennuple in
V_{n}
. So
\textit{{t}}_{\bm{h}}
is orthogonal to
N

i.e,

t^{\alpha}_{h}\cdot N_{\alpha}=0.

Taking co-variant derivative with respect to
y^{\beta}
we have

t^{\alpha}_{h;\beta}N_{\alpha}+N_{\alpha;\beta}t^{\alpha}_{h}=0.

Multiplying both side by
t^{\beta}_{h}
we get

\left(t^{\alpha}_{h;\beta}t^{\beta}_{h}\right)N_{\alpha}+\left(N_{\alpha;\beta}t^{\beta}_{h}\right)t^{\alpha}_{h}=0.

\Longrightarrow
Normal component of the 1st curvature of the curve (having tangent
\textit{{e}}_{\bm{h}}
) relative to

V_{n+1}=-
(Tendency of
N^{\alpha}
in the direction of the curve).

i.e.
, Normal curvature of
V_{n}
in the direction of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rightarrow}}{{e_{h}}}

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~=-
Tendency of unit normal
N^{\alpha}
in the direction of
\overrightarrow{\bm{e}}_{{}_{\bm{h}}}
.

Now summing over
h
from
1
to
n
we get

M=-\mbox{div}\,\textit{{N}}.

Theorem 4.17: Prove that conjugate directions in a hypersurface are such that the derived vector of the unit normal in either direction is orthogonal to the other direction.

Proof: From Weingarten’s formula (equation (
4.20
))

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}=-b^{k}_{i}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

Let
a^{i}
and
e^{i}
be unit vectors in the hypersurface
V_{n}
. The derived vector of
N^{\alpha}
in the direction of
a^{i}
is
\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}a^{i}
. So projection of this vector along the direction
e^{i}
is

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)a^{i}\left(a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{h}y^{\beta}e^{h}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b^{k}_{i}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}a^{i}a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{h}y^{\beta}e^{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b^{k}_{i}\left(a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}\nabla_{h}y^{\beta}\right)a^{i}e^{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b^{k}_{i}a_{kh}a^{i}e^{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-b_{ih}a^{i}e^{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~~\mbox{if $a^{i}$ and $e^{i}$ are conjugate directions}.

Hence the theorem.

Note: If
a^{i}=e^{i}
then

\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}e^{i}\left(a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{h}y^{\beta}e^{h}\right)=-b_{ih}e^{i}e^{h}.

Suppose
e^{i}
is tangent to a curve
\gamma
in
V_{n}
. Then
\gamma
will be an asymptotic line of the hypersurface if
b_{ij}e^{i}e^{j}=0
.

Hence,

\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\alpha}\right)e^{i}\left(a_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{h}y^{\beta}e^{h}\right)=0

Thus we have the following result:

“The derived vector of the unit normal along a curve in the hypersurface will be orthogonal to the curve provided the curve is an asymptotic line in the hypersurface”.

4.10

The Gauss and Codazzi Equations on a hypersurface

Theorem 4.18 : The Gauss and Codazzi equations on a hypersurface are given by

R_{ijkl}=\left(b_{jl}b_{ki}-b_{jk}b_{il}\right)+\overline{R}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\gamma}\right)\left(\nabla_{l}y^{\delta}\right)

(4.26)

and

\nabla_{k}b_{ij}-\nabla_{j}b_{ik}=\overline{R}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}N^{\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\gamma}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\delta}\right).

(4.27)

Here
\overline{R}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
is the Riemannian curvature tensor on the enveloping space
V_{n+1}
. The first equation is known as Gauss characteristic equations and second one is called the Codazzi equations.

Proof: We have

g_{ij}=a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right).

(4.28)

As
N^{\alpha}
is the unit normal vector so

a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)N^{\beta}=0.

(4.29)

Also

a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}=1.

(4.30)

Taking covariant derivative of equation (
4.28
) with respect to
x^{k}
we have

\frac{\partial a_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial y^{\gamma}}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\gamma}\right)\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)+a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)+a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)=0.

(4.31)

Now rotating
(i,~j,~k)
cyclically, we get two more equations. Sum of these two equations when substracted from equations (
4.31
), we get

\left[\frac{\partial a_{\beta\gamma}}{\partial y^{\alpha}}+\frac{\partial a_{\gamma\alpha}}{\partial y^{\beta}}-\frac{\partial a_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial y^{\gamma}}\right]\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\gamma}\right)\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)+2a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)=0

\mbox{or,}~~~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\gamma}\right)=0

(Note that the Christoffel symbols of the 1st kind are formed with respect to
a_{\alpha\beta}
evaluated at points of
V_{n}
)

\mbox{or,}~~~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}a_{\mu\gamma}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\beta}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\gamma}\right)=0

\mbox{or,}~~~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}\right)=0

\mbox{or,}~~~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}\right)\left[\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)\right]=0.

Comparing this equation with equation (
4.29
) we can write

\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)=b_{ij}N^{\alpha}.

(4.32)

Now multiply both sides by
a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\beta}
and summing for
\alpha
we obtain

\displaystyle b_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right),

(4.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)+\Gamma_{\mu\nu\beta}N^{\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right).

Taking covariant derivative of equation (
4.29
) with respect to
x^{j}
we get

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)N^{\beta}+a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial a_{\alpha}{\beta}}{\partial y^{\nu}}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\beta},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\Gamma_{\alpha\nu\beta}+\Gamma_{\beta\nu\alpha}\right]\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\beta},

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)N^{\beta}+\Gamma_{\alpha\nu\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\beta}=-\left[a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma_{\beta\nu\alpha}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\beta}\right],

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}\right)N^{\beta}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\beta}

(4.34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\beta\nu}a_{\mu\alpha}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\beta}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)+a_{\alpha\beta}\Gamma^{\beta}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}\right].

Using equation (
4.34
), equation (
4.33
) simplifies to

b_{ij}=-a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left[\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\gamma}\right].

(4.35)

Taking co-variant derivative of equation (
4.30
) with respect to
x^{j}
we have

a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\alpha}\right)N^{\beta}+a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)N^{\alpha}=-\frac{\partial a_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial y^{\nu}}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}.

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~2a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)N^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left[\Gamma_{\nu\alpha\beta}+\Gamma_{\nu\beta\alpha}\right]\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)\left(N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\Gamma_{\nu\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)N^{\alpha}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\alpha}a_{\mu\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\alpha}N^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\nu\mu}a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\mu}N^{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~~(\mu\rightleftharpoons\alpha)

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\nu\mu}a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\mu}N^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0~~~(\alpha\rightleftharpoons\beta)

\displaystyle\mbox{or,}~~~~a_{\alpha\beta}N^{\alpha}\left[\left(\nabla_{i}N^{\beta}\right)+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\nu\mu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)N^{\mu}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(4.36)

Now comparing equation (
4.35
) with equation (
4.29
) we write

\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\mu\nu}(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu})N^{\nu}=\Lambda^{k}_{j}\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}

(4.37)

Using (
4.10
) in equation (
4.35
) we obtain

b_{ij}=-a_{\alpha\beta}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\alpha}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}\right)\Lambda^{k}_{j}=-g_{ik}\Lambda^{k}_{j}=-\Lambda_{ij}~.

Hence equation (
4.10
) can be written as

\nabla_{j}N^{\beta}+\Gamma^{\beta}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\gamma}=-b^{k}_{j}\nabla_{k}y^{\beta}.

(4.38)

From the Ricci identity :

y^{\alpha}_{,ijk}-y^{\alpha}_{,ikj}=y^{\alpha}_{,m}g^{mh}R_{hijk}~,

(4.39)

where
R_{hijk}
is the Riemann curvature tensor in
V_{n}
with respect to the metric
g_{ij}
. Now from equation (
4.32
)

\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}=b_{ij}N^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)

(4.40)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{k}b_{ij}\right)N^{\alpha}+b_{ij}\left(\nabla_{k}N^{\alpha}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\lambda}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)

From (
4.40
),

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{ik}N^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{j}b_{ik}\right)N^{\alpha}+b_{ik}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\alpha}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\lambda}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)

Using (
4.40
)
i.e.,

\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}y^{\alpha}=b_{ik}N^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)

we have,

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{j}b_{ik}\right)N^{\alpha}+b_{ik}\left(\nabla_{j}N^{\alpha}\right)-\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\alpha}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}-\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}\nabla_{j}\right)y^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{\alpha}\left[\nabla_{k}b_{ij}-\nabla_{j}b_{ik}\right]+b_{ij}\left[-b^{l}_{k}\nabla_{l}y^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle-b_{ik}\left[-b^{l}_{j}\nabla_{l}y^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[-\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\lambda}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\nu}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\lambda}\right]\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\sigma}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\sigma}\right)\left[b_{ik}N^{\mu}-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\lambda\sigma}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\sigma}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)\left[b_{ij}N^{\mu}-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\lambda\sigma}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\sigma}\right)\right]

\mbox{or,}~~~\nabla_{m}y^{\alpha}g^{mh}R_{hijk}-N^{\alpha}\{\nabla_{k}b_{ij}-\nabla_{j}b_{ik}\}+b_{ij}b_{kh}g^{mh}\nabla_{m}y^{\alpha}-b_{ik}b_{jh}g^{mh}\nabla_{m}y^{\alpha}

=-b_{ij}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}+b_{ik}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\left[\left(-\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\lambda}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{\nu}}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\mu}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\lambda}\right)\right]

~~~~-b_{ik}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}+b_{ij}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\mu}\right)N^{\nu}

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\mu}_{\lambda\sigma}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\nu}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\sigma}\right)-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\mu}_{\lambda\sigma}\left(\nabla_{i}y^{\lambda}\right)\left(\nabla_{j}y^{\sigma}\right)\left(\nabla_{k}y^{\nu}\right)

\mbox{or,}~~~\left(\nabla_{m}y^{\alpha}\right)g^{mh}\left[R_{hijk}-\left(b_{ik}b_{jh}-b_{ij}b_{kh}\right)\right]-N^{\alpha}\left(\nabla_{k}b_{ij}-\nabla_{j}b_{ki}\right)
